- **Invariant:** none violated. `I5` is the relevant frame (recovery *was* bounded — 0.16-0.43 s after the lock released) and this is filed as the risk, not as a failure.
- **Severity:** minor (observation — as-designed structure, unbounded blast radius)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-6-schedule-duplicate-fire.md`, Part C2 (the row lock held for 60 s with **no** kill, so nothing is confounded by failover). Raw evidence: `w2-6/partC2-stall.txt`, `w2-6/partC2-lock.txt`, `w2-6/final-census.txt`.
- **Observed:** DB clock.

  ```
  01:16:42.902  psql: BEGIN; SELECT ... FROM schedules WHERE name='edge-every-minute' FOR UPDATE
  01:16:51.164  scheduler fires; run cfc81283 created (Pending)
  01:16:51.695  pg_stat_activity: leader pid=15707 ip=172.20.0.3 blocked Lock/transactionid
  01:16:52.001  UNRELATED run 0fea5f6f created via POST /api/v1/runs (triggered_by='env:UNIFIED_TOKEN')
  01:16:52.407 ... 01:17:41.367   0fea5f6f sampled Pending on a ~2.6 s poll (20 consecutive samples)
  01:17:42.906  psql: ROLLBACK — lock released (held 60.004 s)
  01:17:43.061  cfc81283 -> Running   (+0.155 s after release)
  01:17:43.336  0fea5f6f -> Running   (+0.430 s after release)
  ```

  The API-triggered run — which has nothing to do with any schedule — sat `Pending` for **51.335 s** (`01:16:52.001` -> `01:17:43.336`) and the schedule's own run for **51.897 s**, both for the entire remainder of the lock hold. The cause is structural: `checkAndFireSchedules` is called **synchronously from the tick loop** (`internal/controller/scheduler.go:72`) *after* `TransitionPendingToQueued` (`:58`), so a blocked `UPDATE` inside it stops the loop, and the loop is the only thing that promotes `Pending` -> `Queued` cluster-wide (`docs/high-availability.md:81`, `:159`). The wait is **unbounded**: `statement_timeout` and `lock_timeout` are both `0` on the server (verified live, `w2-6/final-census.txt`) and neither string appears anywhere in `internal/`, `cmd/` or `test/ha/`, while the `Exec` carries the scheduler goroutine's process-lifetime ctx. Nothing is logged for the duration — the leader holds its advisory lock throughout, so `pg_locks` shows a healthy leader and no failover occurs.
- **Expected:** The mechanism is a reasonable single-threaded design, and this stays an observation — but the "no contract is contradicted" verdict is **borderline and the declined reading deserves to be on the record rather than waved past.** `docs/high-availability.md:159` says "Only **Pending->Queued transitions and schedule fires are paused** while there is no leader", and that sentence can be read as an implicit promise in the other direction: that while there *is* a healthy leader, those two things are not paused. Here a leader that was demonstrably healthy — holding its advisory lock throughout, visible as the sole holder in `pg_locks`, never failing over — paused `Pending`->`Queued` cluster-wide for **51.3 s**. On the biconditional reading that is a contradiction, and it would be a documented-contract minor of exactly the shape W2-1 filed against the adjacent leader-election prose (`FINDINGS.md:515`, on `:75-77`/`:94`/`:102-105`/`:382-384`). **Why this entry declines that reading:** `:159` sits under the heading "### What happens during leader absence" (`:157`), so its subject is which subsystems degrade *during an outage*, and the word "only" is doing scoping work within that subject — it bounds the blast radius of leader absence, it does not assert a liveness property of leader presence. Nothing elsewhere in the document promises a bound on `Pending`->`Queued` latency under a healthy leader (the failover estimates at `:146-150` are about lock handover, not about the loop's throughput once held), so there is no second passage to corroborate the stronger reading. A reviewer who takes the biconditional reading anyway should reclassify this as a documented-contract minor; the observation label is a judgement about the sentence's scope, not a claim that the behaviour is harmless. What is worth recording either way is the amplification: a *row-level* contention event on one row of a table that is otherwise written once per minute per schedule becomes a **cluster-wide scheduling outage of unbounded duration**, with no leader failover (the scheduler still holds its advisory lock, so no other replica takes over) and no log line. Any of a long-running `ALTER TABLE schedules`, a backup tool taking row locks, an operator's forgotten `BEGIN` in a psql session, or a `POST /api/v1/schedules` inside a longer transaction reaches the same state.
- **Notes:** Cheap mitigations, in increasing cost: set a `lock_timeout`/`statement_timeout` on the scheduler's connection so the write fails fast — noting that this converts the stall into the *other* failure in this scenario, the un-retried `:195` Warn, which is worse unless `:189`-`:196` is made atomic first; or move `checkAndFireSchedules` off the tick loop into its own goroutine so a blocked schedule write cannot stop `Pending`->`Queued`. **Related but distinct:** **W2-9's** head-of-line finding is about the *contents* of the Pending snapshot (`FINDINGS.md:1398`); this is about the loop that reads it not running at all. (This sentence originally read "W2-4's ... (`FINDINGS.md:779`, `:865`)", written before W2-9 existed. Those two are W2-4 entries about the *queued-run reaper*, and `:865` — a run that spends its grace blocked on a mutex while `Pending` — is the *cause* of the backlog W2-9 starves on, not the snapshot finding itself; corrected at the W2 checkpoint.)

## W2-6 — observation: the `CreateRun`->`last_fired_at` window is 1-3 ms wide (38 fires, median 2.0 ms) and 20 aimed `SIGKILL`s did not land in it; every promotion re-checks schedules immediately, and failover measured 0.28-0.47 s

- **Invariant:** none violated. This is the measurement half of the duplicate-fire major above, plus an I5-relevant failover-latency confirmation.
- **Severity:** minor (observation)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-6-schedule-duplicate-fire.md`, Part A step 4 and Part B (Arm A). Raw evidence: `w2-6/armA-kills.txt`, `w2-6/armA-fires.txt`, `w2-6/armA-stats.txt`, `w2-6/promotions.txt`, `w2-6/pg-armA.log.gz`, `w2-6/partA-control.txt`, `w2-6/all-fires.txt`.
- **Observed:**
  - **Window width, measured rather than inferred.** Over the **38** fires captured before Part C, the interval from the `INSERT INTO runs` statement to the `UPDATE schedules SET last_fired_at` statement was **min 1.0 ms, median 2.0 ms, mean 1.95 ms, max 3.0 ms** (histogram: 1 ms x 4, 2 ms x 32, 3 ms x 2 — `w2-6/armA-stats.txt`). The plan and the Task 7 brief both call this "single-digit milliseconds (inferred, not measured)" (`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-30-edge-case-campaign-w2.md:74`); the inference is correct and the measurement tightens it. **Caveat on what the number is:** `log_statement='all'` timestamps each statement at *start*, so this is the gap between the two statements' starts at 1 ms log resolution, not commit-to-commit.
  - **Arm A: 20 attempts, 20 kills delivered, 0 hits.** Each attempt predicted the leader's next check as `promotion + 60k` (justified below), swept a lead time of 0.05-0.60 s to cover docker's kill-delivery latency, `SIGKILL`ed the leader, then restored it. Measured aim: the kill was issued between **-0.515 s and +0.037 s** of the predicted check (median -0.226 s). Verdict per attempt was taken from the Postgres log, not from run counts: across the arm there were **38 fires, 38 distinct occurrence binds, 0 repeated binds and 0 `INSERT`s with no following `UPDATE`**. **Derived, and labelled:** with a ~550 ms aim spread against a 2 ms target the per-attempt hit probability is ~0.4% and ~7% over 20 attempts, so 0/20 is the unremarkable outcome — **this is "not reproduced live", not evidence that the window is unreachable.** The same state was then produced deterministically in Part C by widening the window instead of sharpening the aim, which is the precedent W2-3's Arm D (0/10, `FINDINGS.md:741`) and W2-5 both set.
  - **A freshly promoted leader re-checks schedules immediately, and this is why the duplicate lands in seconds.** `lastScheduleCheck` is a **local variable** of `RunScheduler` (`internal/controller/scheduler.go:31`), zero at process start, and the gate is `t.Sub(lastScheduleCheck) >= time.Minute` (`:71`), so the first tick after promotion always checks. Observed on **every promotion in the session — 22 of them**, each followed by a `LIST schedules` within one 200 ms tick. **Citation precision:** `w2-6/promotions.txt` holds **21** of those lines (the initial election at `00:34:46.105` plus one per Arm A kill); the 22nd is Part C's promotion, which post-dates that capture and is evidenced instead by the `LIST 01:13:50.753 h=172.20.0.3` line in `w2-6/all-fires.txt`, 0.394 s after the `SIGKILL`. **Note that 21 is also the session's total controller `SIGKILL` count** (20 Arm A + 1 Part C) — the two numbers differ by one because the initial election was not preceded by a kill, and an earlier draft conflated them. Because this schedule was permanently in backlog (see the companion major), a promotion that landed *after* that minute's check had already run also fired one extra catch-up occurrence: **8** pairs of fires **123-372 ms** apart with **different** occurrence binds appear in `w2-6/armA-fires.txt` (an earlier draft said 5 pairs at 198-372 ms), which is the control that proves an ordinary failover does *not* duplicate. Those 8 extra fires are also what kept the schedule alive through the arm — each cut ~59.7 s off its lag, total −478.156 s; see the companion major's Observed (2b).
  - **Failover after `SIGKILL`: 0.280-0.470 s, median 0.344 s, n=20** (kill issued on the host clock -> `"scheduler became leader"` on the new leader's clock, so this figure crosses clocks and carries the ~0.3 s command-latency caveat as an upper bound on its own accuracy). `docs/high-availability.md:133` — the `kill -9` row of the lock-release table — predicts "Usually within a few seconds", and the estimate block at `:146-150` gives "well under a second to a few seconds" end to end; the measured value is comfortably inside both. (**Corrected citation:** an earlier draft cited `:107-110` for that quote, which is the "### Normal operation (200ms tick)" heading, not the failover table. The quote is genuine; the line reference was not.) `docs/high-availability.md:81`'s single-leader property also held throughout — `pg_locks` never showed more than one holder of `0x65786364` (= `1702388580` decimal).
- **Expected:** All four measurements match the documented or code-read design; none is a defect. They are recorded because the duplicate-fire major depends on the first (a 2 ms window is why the honest arm is a widened one) and the third (an immediate re-check is why the successor re-fires within seconds rather than a minute later), and because the second is the mandated report of an arm that missed.
- **Notes:**
  - **Instrument note for later waves.** The `UPDATE schedules SET last_fired_at = $1` bind parameter is the **only** surface in the whole system that names which cron occurrence a fire was for; `runs.triggered_by` carries only `schedule:<name>`. Any scenario reasoning about schedule correctness therefore needs Postgres statement logging (`log_statement='all'`, `log_line_prefix='%m [%p] h=%h '`, `log_parameter_max_length` is `-1` by default on `postgres:16-alpine`), and the extractor used here is `w2-6/fires.py` (in repo at `test/edgecase/tools/w2/w2-6-fires.py`). **Two gotchas it exposed:** the `INSERT` statement is logged across several physical lines and only the first carries the timestamp prefix, so continuation lines must be merged before classifying; and a probe query that *mentions* `UPDATE schedules SET last_fired_at` inside a string literal is itself logged and will be mis-classified (they appear in `w2-6/all-fires.txt` as `occ=None` and are not fires). Cost on this rig: ~114 MB of Postgres log for 48 minutes on a 3-replica stack (~3.4 MB gzipped).
  - **`pg_locks` for the scheduler key worked exactly as W2-1 established** — one row, continuously, correctly identifying the holder by `client_addr` — and was the leader-identification instrument for all 20 attempts plus Parts C and D. The decimal form of `0x65786364` is **1702388580**; an earlier draft of this scenario's gate used `1702389604`, which is simply wrong arithmetic and made the census read zero holders. Anyone re-deriving it should check against `SELECT objid FROM pg_locks WHERE locktype='advisory'` rather than trusting the conversion.
  - **`docker compose logs -t <one service>` still prefixes each line with the service name**, so `awk '{print $1}'` yields `controller2-1`, not the timestamp; `--no-log-prefix` is required. Two Arm A batches were lost to this and to `--tail 500` not reaching a promotion line that had scrolled out. Neither cost an attempt (no kill was issued in either), but both cost wall time.

## W2-6 — resolved branch-internal asset bug: the schedule fixture is documented as a `POST /api/v1/jobs` payload and returns 400 there; schedules have their own endpoint

- **Classification:** branch-internal asset defect, fixed on this branch. Reported outside both the violation and observation tallies, per the W1 checkpoint's third bucket.
- **Severity:** n/a (campaign asset)
- **Repro:** `curl -X POST localhost:18080/api/v1/jobs --data-binary @test/edgecase/workloads/schedule-every-minute.payload.json` -> **400**. Raw evidence: `w2-6/baseline-gate.txt`.
- **Observed:** `test/edgecase/README.md` stated "Every `*.payload.json` is the pre-encoded `{"yaml":"..."}` body for `POST /api/v1/jobs`" and listed `schedule-every-minute.payload.json` in that table; the W2 plan's Task 7 and the Task 7 brief repeated it. The jobs handler unmarshals the body into `dsl.Spec`, which has no `cron`/`job` members, so the request fails at parse time with:

  ```
  invalid yaml: yaml: unmarshal errors:
    line 6: field cron not found in type dsl.Spec
    line 7: field job not found in type dsl.Spec
  ```

  The correct endpoint is **`POST /api/v1/schedules`** (`internal/controller/server.go:424`), which returned `200` and `{"name":"edge-every-minute","cron":"* * * * *","jobName":"edge-tick",...}`.
- **Expected:** The fixture itself is correct; the documentation of how to apply it was not. The schedule fixture was added in Task 1 and W2-6 is the first scenario to consume it, so the error had never been executed before.
- **Notes:**
  - Fixed in `test/edgecase/README.md` (the "Workload fixtures" paragraph and the fixture table row now both name `POST /api/v1/schedules` and quote the 400), and the W2-6 runbook's baseline gate uses the right path.
  - **A second, load-bearing fact discovered while fixing it:** `UpsertSchedule` (`internal/store/postgres.go:2108-2122`) does **not** touch `last_fired_at` in its `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` list, so re-applying a schedule preserves it. `DELETE /api/v1/schedules/{name}` followed by `POST` is therefore the only way to return a schedule to its birth state — which is what made Part D's "no DB mutation" claim possible, and is worth knowing for any later scenario that needs a schedule reset.

## W2-7 — two live agent processes sharing one agent ID destroy each other's work: the startup reconcile has no grace and no ownership check, so it terminalizes runs a live process is still executing
- **Invariant:** **I1 (run accounting)** — "every API-accepted run reaches exactly one terminal state; no phantom runs from duplicate fires/webhooks" (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:48`) — **with I7 (`...:54`) as a second limb.** **The fit needs stating precisely rather than loosely:** the run *does* reach exactly one terminal state, so I1's literal first clause is not what breaks. What breaks is the run-accounting property the clause protects — the terminal state recorded is **false**, and it was written by a path that had no evidence the work had stopped. **The in-campaign precedent for exactly this reading is W2-3 (`FINDINGS.md:687`)**, which files the identical shape — a healthy, heartbeating agent's `Running` run failed by an internal controller path, with no fault injected at the agent — as **I1 / major**, and that entry stands accepted. **Do not cite the W1 checkpoint's `:484` here — an earlier version of this sentence did, and `:484` says the opposite for its own shape** ("major, I7 (I1 held) ... the state it reaches is simply wrong"), because W1-5's work had already completed when its record was falsified. The discriminator, and the rule that governs both, is stated at the W2 checkpoint (`FINDINGS.md:1484`): a wrong record over work that has already stopped is I7; a wrong record that terminates work still in flight is I1. **I7 is the second limb** for the interval in which the DB reads `Failed` with `exit_code NULL` while the step is demonstrably still executing (1.798 s and 0.963 s here, measured in the I6 observation below). An earlier version of this entry made the documented contract the *primary* attribution; that was wrong and is corrected in Expected.
- **Severity:** major
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-7-duplicate-agent-id.md`, Parts A1 and A2. Stack = `test/ha` + `../edgecase/compose/oneway.override.yaml` + `../edgecase/compose/dupagent.override.yaml` (the new overlay adds `agent1b`, a fourth agent service identical to `agent1` including `--id agent1` and the same credential file in the same named volume). `agent2` stopped for the whole scenario so every run is claimed by an `agent1`-ID process. Evidence: `w2-7/gate.txt`, `w2-7/gate-config-agents.txt`, `w2-7/g7-control.txt`, `w2-7/partA1.txt`, `w2-7/partA2.txt`, `w2-7/agent1-full.log`, `w2-7/agent1b-full.log`, `w2-7/controllers-full.log`.
- **Observed:**
  - **The control first, and it is a real control.** Run `e05842ef` was claimed by `agent1` at `02:33:53.611` and sampled 11 times over the next 193.6 s: `Running` throughout, `updated_at` frozen at `02:33:53.623`, no reconcile of any kind (`w2-7/g7-control.txt`); it was still `Running` at `02:37:32` (age 219.7 s). `docker compose --profile dup ps -a` in the same capture lists **no `agent1b` row at all** — the container had never been created at that point, so the control window contains no armed injection (the specific mistake W2-5 had to correct).
  - **A1 — the twin kills a 4-minute-old run 1.9 s after being started.** `docker compose up -d agent1b` issued at host `02:37:49.948`; the twin logged `"enrollment token rejected (expired or already consumed); continuing with the existing credential"` at `02:37:51.847`, `"agent registered"` at `02:37:51.863`, and `"failed orphaned runs left by previous agent process" count=1` at `02:37:51.871`. `controller3` logged `"agent reconcile: failed orphaned run (agent process replaced)"` for `e05842ef` at `02:37:51.871` (`api_agent.go:828`), and the run's `updated_at` is `02:37:51.867` — **238.256 s after `claimed_at`**, i.e. the run had been executing for four minutes.
  - **A2 — and it kills a 4-second-old run just as readily.** Run `7b7ffb9d` claimed by `agent1` at `02:39:11.785` (attributed from `agent1`'s own `"running"` line at `02:39:11.793`); `docker compose restart agent1b` at host `02:39:14.882`; run `Failed` at `02:39:15.833` — **4.048 s** after `claimed_at`. `ListRunningRunIDsByAgent` (`internal/store/postgres.go:266-285`) is a bare `SELECT id FROM runs WHERE claimed_by = $1 AND status = 'Running'` at `:271` with no time clause of any kind, so no age protects a run here.
  - **The endpoint applies no ownership check whatsoever.** `handleAgentReconcileRuns` (`internal/controller/api_agent.go:815-832`) takes the agent id from the **URL path** and calls neither `agentRunGuard` nor anything else — code-read, and Parts A1/A2 are its live demonstration: a process that had never claimed anything terminated two runs it never executed.
  - **The step row is left with a NULL exit code, and `MarkRunStepsInterrupted` writes `'Failed'`, not `'Interrupted'`.** Both runs' `step_reports` rows read `Failed | exit_code NULL | ended_at = <the reap instant>` (`w2-7/partA1.txt`, `w2-7/partA2.txt`) — verified against `postgres.go:819-830`, whose `UPDATE` sets `status = 'Failed'`. A NULL `exit_code` on a `Failed` step is the fingerprint of this path; the status string is not. (The W2-7 runbook's first draft predicted `'Interrupted'` and is corrected.)
  - **The root cause is the absence of any fencing, and the operationally dangerous part is that the collision is invisible** — filed as its own observation below.
- **Expected:** **The docs are silent on this configuration, not contradicted by it — and the honest basis is I1, not a contract.** `docs/high-availability.md:408-411` states the premise the startup reconcile rests on: "The reaper above cannot see one case: the agent process is **replaced** (SIGKILL, OOM, container restart) and the new process re-registers under the **same agent ID** within `staleAfter`. The heartbeat resumes immediately, so runs claimed by the previous process incarnation never look stuck — **yet nothing is executing them anymore.**" That passage is scoped **entirely** to the *replaced-process* case, and its closing clause is a premise **about that case**, not a universal claim — so a concurrent twin does not falsify a promise, it exercises a case the passage never contemplated. **And the passage tells against a contract reading rather than for it:** its only behavioural sentence, `:414-415` — "the controller fails every `Running` run still claimed by that agent ID" — *describes and sanctions precisely what was observed here*, so citing it would mean citing the sentence that authorises the behaviour. `grep`ping `docs/` finds nothing addressing two concurrent processes under one agent ID at all. **The violation therefore rests on I1 (with I7), on the W2-3 precedent (`FINDINGS.md:687`) for the identical shape** — a run's recorded terminal state written by a path with no evidence the work had stopped — and the severity is unaffected by the change of limb. `handleAgentReconcileRuns`' own doc comment (`api_agent.go:808-814`) repeats the sequential-replacement premise and is in any case not citable as a contract per `FINDINGS.md:479`.
- **Notes:**
  - **A2 on its own would be an observation, not a violation.** `postgres.go:271` is ungraced by construction and W2-2 already filed the no-grace behaviour as an observation (`FINDINGS.md:612`, 2.409 s measured; 4.048 s here is an independent second measurement). What makes this entry a violation is that a **live** process's run was terminalized on no evidence the work had stopped (I1), not the missing grace — do not re-file the grace. (An earlier version of this note said "the false premise"; the premise at `:411` is scoped to sequential replacement and is not the basis — see Expected.)
  - **Fix shape.** The cheapest correct fix is a fencing token: give the agent a per-process nonce at register time, store it on `agents` and alongside `runs.claimed_by`, and make both `handleAgentReconcileRuns` and the heartbeat reconcile scope their `failOrphanedRun` calls to runs claimed under *that* nonce. Without it, `claimed_by` cannot distinguish two processes and no amount of grace tuning helps.
  - `docs/agents.md:41-48` documents several agents sharing a host and is careful that each has a **distinct** ID ("Several auto-generated agents can share a host because each persisted credential is scoped to its returned ID... A process never removes another ID's credential"). Nothing anywhere says one ID must be held by exactly one process — see the invisibility observation below.

## W2-7 — with both processes live, each heartbeat reap kills the *other* process's run: two legitimate runs destroyed 1.682 s apart, and neither the controller nor either agent logs a single line about it
- **Invariant:** **I1 (run accounting)**, quoted above (`...:48`), on the same "the terminal state recorded is false" reading, **with I7 (`...:54`) as a second limb** for the 2.026 s / 3.392 s intervals in which the DB read `Failed` / `exit_code NULL` while each step was still executing. Same precedent as the entry above: W2-3 (`FINDINGS.md:687`) files this shape as I1 / major. **And this entry is strictly worse than that precedent on both of its axes** — W2-3 had to induce its precondition with a direct `DELETE` and its reap emits a `WARN` naming the run, whereas this needs **no injection of any kind** (two ordinary processes, no fault) and emits **nothing anywhere**. If W2-3 is accepted at major, this cannot be less. **I3 is NOT violated** — campaign I3 is "*no lock leaks* — mutex/semaphore/concurrency slots are released when the holder reaches a terminal state" (`...:50`) — and it is discussed as a separate observation below because what happened is the *opposite* of a leak. (W2-5's entry had to be corrected for exactly this mislabel; not repeated here.)
- **Severity:** major
- **Repro:** same scenario, Part B. Evidence: `w2-7/partB.txt`, `w2-7/detector.txt`, `w2-7/agent1-full.log`, `w2-7/agent1b-full.log`, `w2-7/controllers-full.log`, `w2-7/sideeffect.log`.
- **Observed:**
  - Two runs, one per process, each attributed from its own container's `"running"` line (the **only** attribution instrument available — `claimed_by` is the string `agent1` for both): `33a02f4a` (`edge-sideeffect`, mutex `edge-mutex`) claimed `02:40:07.867`, executed by **`agent1`** (`02:40:07.874`); `0384cca0` (`edge-longrun`) claimed `02:40:10.917`, executed by **`agent1b`** (`02:40:10.922`).
  - Both were failed a little over one minute later, **1.682 s apart**: `33a02f4a` at `02:41:15.854` (**67.987 s** after its claim), `0384cca0` at `02:41:17.536` (**66.619 s** after its claim). Each process was still executing its own run at that moment (see the fence measurements in the I6 observation below).
  - **Nothing logged it.** `api_agent.go:101-119`'s heartbeat reconcile emits only on `failOrphanedRun` *error* (`slog.Warn` at `:115`), so a successful mutual annihilation is completely silent. Attribution therefore had to be built by elimination, and every limb is evidenced in `w2-7/partB.txt`:
    - **not the startup reconcile** — `grep -c "agent process replaced"` over the whole session's controller logs is **2**, and both lines are Part A's (`02:37:51.871`, `02:39:15.836`); there is none at `02:41:15`/`02:41:17`;
    - **not the stuck-run reaper** — `grep -c "stuck-run reaper"` over the same logs was **0** at that point, and it could not have matched anyway: the `agents` row was present throughout and `NOW() - last_seen_at`, sampled every 5 s across the window, read **0.27-13.21 s**, so both disjuncts of `(a.id IS NULL OR a.last_seen_at < NOW()-90s)` (`postgres.go:1238-1248`) were false;
    - **not the queued-run reaper** — 0 lines, and it selects only `Queued` (`postgres.go:1275-1286`);
    - **not a cancel** — `GET /api/v1/audit` contains **0** `run.cancel` rows;
    - **positively**, the two terminal writes are 1.682 s apart, which is a two-independent-heartbeat signature (15 s ticker, `internal/agent/heartbeat.go:10`) and not the stuck-run reaper's grid, whose executions cluster within ~14 ms (measured in this same session: `w2-7/partD-sweeps.txt`).
  - **The mechanism is exactly symmetric and needs no third run**: `MaxConcurrent` defaults to 1 (`internal/agent/agent.go:217-220`), each process reports only its own in-flight set (`activeRuns.Snapshot`, `agent.go:277`), and `ListReconcilableRunIDsByAgent` (`postgres.go:287-309`, predicate at `:294`) returns every `Running` run under the shared ID older than the 60 s `heartbeatReconcileGrace` (`api_agent.go:71`). Each snapshot omits the other's run, so each heartbeat kills it.
  - **This is a steady state, not a transient.** No injection, no restart and no fault of any kind occurred between `02:40:07` and `02:41:17`; the only "fault" is that two processes were running.
- **Expected:** **Silent, not contradicted — the same analysis as the previous entry, and it applies with more force here.** `docs/high-availability.md:408-411` is scoped to sequential replacement, and its one behavioural sentence (`:414-415`, "the controller fails every `Running` run still claimed by that agent ID") sanctions failing `Running` runs under the shared ID, so the passage cannot be cited against this behaviour; nothing in `docs/` contemplates two concurrent processes under one agent ID. The violation rests on **I1** per the W2-3 precedent (`FINDINGS.md:687`), with **I7** as the second limb. `api_agent.go:79-84`'s own statement of the grace's purpose ("Protects a run the agent only just claimed from being reaped before the agent's next heartbeat has had a chance to report it as active") is precise about the code and therefore, per `FINDINGS.md:479`, **not** citable as a contract; it is quoted only to show the 60 s grace is designed against a *timing* hazard and offers no protection at all against a *second reporter*.
- **Notes:**
  - **Severity reasoning, stated because the Part A and Part B entries could be collapsed and should not be.** Part A needs an operator action (starting/restarting a process) and produces one loud controller log line per victim. Part B needs **no action at all** and produces **none**, so it is the shape that would survive undetected in production: an operator whose Ansible/systemd/k8s config accidentally starts two agents with one ID sees runs failing at roughly one per minute with `exit_code NULL`, no reason on the run, and nothing in any log naming a cause.
  - **A cheap diagnosability fix independent of the fencing fix:** log the heartbeat reconcile's successes, not only its errors (`api_agent.go:110-117`), matching the startup path's `:828` and the reaper's `stuckrun_reaper.go:62` (an earlier draft said `:64`, which is the `if len(ids) > 0 {` summary guard; the per-run `slog.Warn("stuck-run reaper: failed orphaned run (agent lost)")` is at `:62`). That alone converts this from silent to greppable. W2-2 already filed the sibling gap that a reconcile-failed run carries no operator-visible reason (`FINDINGS.md:652`); the same fix applies and is not re-filed here.

## W2-7 — the ownership guard is a bare string compare against `claimed_by`, so **any local reader of an agent's credential file** can write to, and terminalize, a run that agent is executing — **no duplicate ID is required**: a third client injected a log line and a fabricated step, then forged `Succeeded` on a real run 49.1 s into a ~300 s step, **and the forged terminal write left no audit row at all**
- **Invariant:** I7 (state display consistency) — "run status, approval status, and **audit rows** never contradict each other or reality" (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:54`) — is the literal fit, and it is violated on **both** of its named surfaces: a run recorded `Succeeded` with 52 log rows against a workload that emits ~300, carrying a step row that still says `Running` plus a step row for a step that never existed; **and no audit row anywhere records the write that did it** (see the audit bullet in Observed). I1 (`...:48`) is the secondary limb, on the same "terminal state recorded is false" reading as the entries above.
- **Severity:** major — the explicit critical-vs-major paragraph is in the Notes below ("Scope honestly"), stated rather than assumed because this is the one entry in the wave whose subject matter falls under the rubric's "security" word (`:6`).
- **Repro:** same scenario, Part C. Evidence: `w2-7/partC.txt`, `w2-7/partC-cred.json`, `w2-7/partC-token.json` (both redacted — the rig's `agent-credentials` volume was destroyed by `down -v`, so the tokens are dead), `w2-7/agent1b-full.log`.
- **Observed:**
  - **State the arm's real precondition first, because it is *not* the duplicate ID.** Everything below needs exactly two things: **one** agent process executing a run, and **any** local reader of that agent's credential file. The duplicate-ID rig supplied the reader conveniently (a second container mounting the same volume) and supplied a *second* process whose run could be attacked, but neither is load-bearing: `agentRunGuard` compares the caller's authenticated agent ID against `runs.claimed_by`, and a client holding agent X's credential satisfies that compare for **every** run X has claimed — including the run X is executing right now, in a single-process deployment. **The real-deployment equivalent is any process on the agent host that can read `$HOME/.unified-cd/<id>/credential.json`.** This is stated up front so the entry is not triaged as a duplicate-ID artifact that a fencing token closes: a per-process nonce fixes Parts A/B, but a nonce held in the same readable file does **not** fix this unless the write path also binds it to something the reader cannot obtain.
  - **Getting a credential took one `cat`.** `agent1`'s rotating refresh credential lives in the `agent-credentials` **named volume** at `/var/lib/unified-cd-agent/agent1/credentials.json`; the `agent-enroll` service mounts the same volume and has a shell, so `docker compose run --rm --no-deps -T agent-enroll sh -c 'cat ...'` returned it. `POST /api/v1/agents/token/refresh` with it as the bearer answered **200** with a fresh `accessToken` (1 h TTL, `internal/controller/api_agent_enrollment.go:21`) — `w2-7/partC-token.json`.
  - **The victim run was executed by a different process.** `7d7e4ad5` was claimed at `02:55:58.552` with `claimed_by = 'agent1'`, and `agent1b`'s container log carries its `"running"` line at `02:55:58.556` — so the executor was `agent1b` and the writer below is a third, unrelated client.
  - **`POST /api/v1/agents/agent1/logs` → 204.** The injected line appears in the victim's own stream at `seq 435`, immediately after `seq 434` `"tick 28"` written by `agent1b` at `02:56:26.561`: `{"seq":435,...,"timestamp":"2026-07-30T02:56:27Z","line":"w2-7-third-process-marker"}`.
  - **`POST /api/v1/agents/agent1/steps` → 204.** A `step_reports` row for a step index the job does not have was created: `7 | w2-7-injected-step | Running`.
  - **`POST /api/v1/agents/agent1/runs/7d7e4ad5.../finish` with `{"status":"Succeeded"}` → 204**, at host `02:56:47.034`; the run flipped `Running → Succeeded` with `updated_at 02:56:47.627` — **49.1 s** into a step whose workload runs ~300 s. **Basis, stated because an earlier version of this entry said 48.6 s and no capture yields that number:** `updated_at 02:56:47.627 − claimed_at 02:55:58.552 = 49.075 s`, both DB-clock, which is the defensible figure; the host-clock POST at `02:56:47.034` against the same `claimed_at` gives 48.482 s across two clocks. Step 0's own `started_at` was **not** captured in `w2-7/partC.txt`, so "into the step" is measured from the claim, which precedes it by milliseconds. `agent1b`'s cancel poller noticed at `02:56:48.566` and interrupted. Final state: run `Succeeded`; step `0 | tick | Running | exit_code NULL | ended_at NULL`; step `7 | w2-7-injected-step | Running`; **52** log rows.
  - **No audit row records any of it — the agent identity routes carry no audit middleware at all** (code-read; **not** measured, and the omission is deliberate to record: no capture queried `GET /api/v1/audit` after Part C, so this limb is a read of the router, not an observation). `auditLogMiddleware` (`internal/controller/audit.go:163-172`) documents itself as recording "state-changing (POST/PUT/DELETE) **human** API calls", and `classifyAudit`'s own comment (`:155-157`) says routes are "intentionally excluded such as agent/webhook-ingress/auth endpoints". It is mounted on exactly four human subrouters — `internal/controller/server.go:357` (`/api/v1` under `ServerAuth`), `:417` (webhooks), `:426` (schedules), `:435` (appsources) — whereas every agent identity route is registered directly on `s.r` from `agentRouteIdentityMatrix` (`server.go:242-261`, registration loop `:263-282`) with only `agentAuth` + `requireAgentPathIdentity` in front of it. `POST /api/v1/agents/{agentId}/runs/{runId}/finish` is in that matrix (`:251`), so the write that forged a terminal status on a real deployment run is **absent from the audit trail entirely** — not merely unattributable between two processes holding one ID. Since this entry's primary invariant is I7, whose text names audit rows explicitly, that is part of the violation and not a side note.
  - **The guard that let all three through** is `agentRunGuard` (`internal/controller/agent_guard.go:100-131`), whose entire ownership test is `run.ClaimedBy == "" || run.ClaimedBy != agentID` at **`:121`** — a string compare, with a `claimedBy` memo cache in front of it. Because both agent processes (and this third client) authenticate as `agent1` and `claimed_by` is the literal string `agent1`, the guard **cannot distinguish them even in principle**. `handleAgentFinishRun` calls the guard with `rejectTerminal = false`, so a *non*-terminal run is a legitimate finish target for any such caller.
  - **This is the same defect surface as Parts A/B seen from the write side**, and it is why a fencing token is the fix rather than better authorization: the credential legitimately belongs to `agent1`, and every check the controller makes is satisfied.
- **Expected:** nothing in `docs/` scopes an agent credential to one process or one run. `docs/agents.md:41-48` is explicit that several agents may share a host and careful that each has a distinct ID, but says nothing about one ID across processes. The invariant that *is* violated is about the outcome, not the authorization: a run's recorded status must not disagree with its own step/log rows, **and a state-changing write must not be invisible to the audit trail** — both halves of I7's text, both broken here.
- **Notes:**
  - **Scope honestly.** This is **not** a privilege-escalation finding: the third client held `agent1`'s own credential, read from a volume only the rig's own services mount. The finding is that **the credential is the only fence** — and, per the first Observed bullet, that fence is equally thin in a single-process deployment, so this must not be triaged as duplicate-ID fallout. Credential sharing, which the `agent-credentials` named volume makes the *natural* way to run a duplicate, is indistinguishable from legitimate operation. **Critical versus major, argued explicitly — this file's rubric (`:6`) names data loss, silent corruption and *security*, and this is the wave's one entry the third word reaches, so the judgement must be on the page the way `:1308` and `:1344` put theirs.** For **critical**: the write forges a *terminal* status on a real, executing run; it is accepted with `204` and **leaves no audit row anywhere**, because every agent identity route is registered without `auditLogMiddleware` (`server.go:242-282` versus `:357`/`:417`/`:426`/`:435`), so the one artifact that exists to answer "who changed this" is silent by construction; the resulting record is internally contradictory (a `Succeeded` run whose only real step still reads `Running`, plus a step row for an index the job does not have, plus 52 of ~300 log rows); and the precondition on a real deployment is one local read of `$HOME/.unified-cd/<id>/credential.json`, not a stolen secret from elsewhere. **Held at major nonetheless, and the reason is that no privilege boundary is crossed.** The third client presented `agent1`'s own credential and every check the controller made was correctly satisfied — `agentAuth` authenticated a genuine agent identity and `agentRunGuard`'s compare (`agent_guard.go:121`) matched a run that identity really had claimed. Nothing was escalated, impersonated across a trust boundary, or reached without the credential; the finding is that **the credential is the only fence**, which is a design gap of the same kind as the missing fencing nonce in Parts A/B rather than an authorization bypass. Nor is anything destroyed that the legitimate agent could not itself have destroyed with the same call. **The two measurements that would flip it, neither taken:** (i) whether a *non*-privileged local process — one that could not already act as the agent, e.g. a different uid on the agent host, or a step's own user code running inside a job the agent executes — can read that credential file, which turns "the credential is the only fence" into a genuine privilege boundary crossed; and (ii) whether the missing audit row is reachable on a **human** route as well, which would move it from an agent-plane design decision (`classifyAudit`'s own comment says agent endpoints are "intentionally excluded") to an audit-trail defect proper. Both are cheap and both are the right follow-up if this entry is triaged toward critical.
  - **The three writes have different fix shapes.** The fabricated step index and the log line are cured by a fencing nonce on the write path. The `finish` is worse: it needs the nonce *and* it needs the success path to terminalize the run's step rows the way `failOrphanedRun` does via `MarkRunStepsInterrupted` — otherwise even a legitimate agent's finish can leave a `Succeeded` run with a `Running` step. That second half is the same shape as the already-filed W2-2 minor (`FINDINGS.md:587`, "a step permanently reporting `Running` under a terminal run") reached from a third direction; cross-referenced, not re-filed.
  - **Rotation cost, measured.** The exchange at `02:56:02` rotated the shared refresh credential. Neither live process logged a single auth failure in the following 3 minutes (`grep -ciE "unauthor|401|obtain agent token"` = 0 on both, `w2-7/partC.txt`) — expected, because both were holding 1 h access tokens and `agentRefreshOverlap` keeps a superseded refresh token usable. Two processes sharing one rotating credential file is nonetheless a real hazard on a longer horizon: `loadRefreshCredential` returns early once `m.loaded` is set (`internal/agent/credentials.go:289-292`), so neither process re-reads the other's rotation from disk. The file's `refreshExpiresAt` when read in Part C was `2026-08-29T02:48:23Z` — exactly `agent1b`'s registration instant — i.e. **starting the twin had already rotated the credential out from under `agent1`** (`w2-7/partC-cred.json`).

## W2-7 — the stuck-run reaper's deleted-`agents`-row branch is reachable with no DB surgery at all: a twin's ordinary SIGTERM deregistration reaped a healthy, heartbeating agent's run 80.1 s into it. **Completes the W2-3 major; do not double-count**
- **Classification:** **violation entry; the defect is counted under W2-3, not additive to the wave tally.** Stated explicitly because the W1 checkpoint's rule (`FINDINGS.md:481`) reads classification off the title and the Severity line, and an entry carrying neither an "observation" marker nor a `Classification:` line is filed by a mechanical reader as a plain, additive violation. The W2 checkpoint must therefore report **two** numbers for W2-7 — *violation entries: 4*, *new violations: 3* — rather than one.
- **Invariant:** I1 (run accounting), quoted above. **This entry does not add a defect.** It supplies the natural trigger and the exposure measurement that the W2-3 entry at `FINDINGS.md:687` ("the stuck-run reaper's `a.id IS NULL` branch has no liveness test at all...") explicitly deferred, having induced its precondition with a direct `DELETE`. Count the defect once, against W2-3.
- **Severity:** major (W2-3's severity, now demonstrated end to end without DB mutation)
- **Repro:** same scenario, Part D, **one aimed attempt, no misses**. Evidence: `w2-7/partD-prep.txt`, `w2-7/partD-upserts.txt`, `w2-7/partD-sweeps.txt`, `w2-7/partD-deletes.txt`, `w2-7/partD-upserts2.txt`, `w2-7/partD-attempt1.txt`, `w2-7/detector.txt`, `w2-7/pg-partD-grids.log.gz`, `w2-7/pg-partD-reap.log.gz`.
- **Observed:**
  - **The two grids, measured before aiming** (twin stopped, `agent1` restarted at host `02:43:39.752` so its 1 + 16 claim pollers all start together — `agent.go:314-327` defaults the detached pool to 16, each long-polling `timeout=30s` at `agent.go:410-414`):
    - **`agent1`'s claim-poll upsert grid** — 6 clusters of **exactly 17** `INSERT INTO agents(id, hostname, os, labels, version, env, last_seen_at) ... ON CONFLICT (id) DO UPDATE` executions (`UpsertAgentOnClaim`, called once per claim HTTP request at `api_agent.go:150`) at `02:43:40.778 / 02:44:10.814 / 02:44:40.854 / 02:45:10.887 / 02:45:40.927 / 02:46:10.964`; within-cluster spread **0.005-0.017 s**, between-cluster period **30.033-30.040 s** (`w2-7/partD-upserts.txt`). *This is the only thing that heals a deleted `agents` row* — `TouchAgent` is a bare `UPDATE` (`postgres.go:1144-1147`) and matches 0 rows.
    - **The stuck-run reaper's sweep grid** — 17 executions of `ListStuckRunIDs`' own `SELECT ... LEFT JOIN agents a ON r.claimed_by = a.id` in **6 clusters** (3/3/2/3/3/3 = 2.83 per cluster) at `02:43:36.460 / 02:44:06.467 / 02:44:36.471 / 02:45:06.473 / 02:45:36.480 / 02:46:06.484`, period **30.002-30.007 s** (`w2-7/partD-sweeps.txt`) — independently reproducing W2-3's and W2-4's clustered-burst correction.
  - **The aim, and it held to ~13 ms.** With the twin restarted (its own upsert family measured at `02:48:23.424 + 30.037k`, distinct from `agent1`'s `02:48:41.165 + 30.038k` — `w2-7/partD-upserts2.txt`), the run was triggered so that `claimed_at` fell ~5 s after an `agent1` cluster, which puts the *last* `agent1` cluster before `claimed_at + 58 s` late in its interval and so maximises the part of the ≤30 s absence window falling after the 60 s claim grace. Predicted: cluster `02:53:11.51`, sweep `02:53:36.53`, healing cluster `02:53:41.55`. Actual: `02:53:11.514`, `02:53:36.543`, `02:53:41.556`.
  - **The timeline, all from `w2-7/partD-attempt1.txt`, `w2-7/detector.txt` and `w2-7/pg-partD-reap.log.gz`:**

    | DB clock | Event | Source |
    |---|---|---|
    | `02:52:16.457` | run `1f0ba2a3` claimed, `claimed_by='agent1'`; executed by **`agent1`** (`agent1` log `02:52:16.462`) | run row + container log |
    | `02:53:11.514` | `agent1` claim-poll upsert cluster, **n=16** (16 not 17 — the normal slot is busy executing the run) | statement log |
    | `02:53:12.821` (host) | `docker compose stop agent1b` | `partD-attempt1.txt` |
    | `02:53:13.118` | twin: `"shutdown signal received; draining in-flight runs"` | `agent1b-full.log` |
    | `02:53:13.122` | **`DELETE FROM agents WHERE id = $1`**; twin logs `"agent deregistered"` at `02:53:13.123` | statement log |
    | `02:53:14.255` | first detector row with `agents=0`; **18** consecutive such rows in all, through `02:53:41.116` (`detector.txt` lines 748-765) | `detector.txt` |
    | `02:53:16.457` | `claimed_at + 60 s` — the run becomes reapable, the row still absent | derived |
    | `02:53:25.851` | `agent1` heartbeat `UPDATE agents SET last_seen_at = NOW() WHERE id = $1` — **0 rows, into a row that does not exist** | statement log |
    | `02:53:36.543/.556/.557` | reaper sweep cluster | statement log |
    | `02:53:36.545` | run **`Failed`**; `controller3`: `"stuck-run reaper: failed orphaned run (agent lost)"` at `02:53:36.549` plus `"count":1` | run row + controller log |
    | `02:53:40.863` | another `agent1` heartbeat, still 0 rows — **the two heartbeats straddle the reap** | statement log |
    | `02:53:41.483` | `agent1`: `"master reported run terminal; interrupting run"` (soft fence, 4.938 s later) | `agent1-full.log` |
    | `02:53:41.556` | `agent1` claim-poll upsert cluster recreates the row; absence ends | statement log |

  - **The two numbers that matter.** The **absence window was 28.434 s — one sample** (`02:53:13.122` → `02:53:41.556`; Part D ran a single aimed attempt, so this is one measurement of a quantity whose spread is not characterised, bounded above by the upsert period and below by 0) — one full upsert period (30.042 s here) less the **1.608 s** by which the SIGTERM followed the preceding cluster. So the exposure the Task 8 brief bounded at "~one claim-poll cycle (~30 s)" is confirmed and tightened to the measured upsert period of **30.033-30.040 s** on a freshly-started agent. And the **run was 80.089 s old** when reaped: the 60 s claim grace plus **20.089 s** of waiting for the next sweep, entirely consistent with W2-3's four measured latencies (19.246 / 28.061 / 11.283 / 8.013 s) being uniform over a 30 s cycle.
  - **The agent was demonstrably healthy at the reap instant.** This is the crux, and it is evidenced three independent ways: (i) two heartbeat `UPDATE`s at `02:53:25.851` and `02:53:40.863` bracket the reap — the process was on its 15 s ticker throughout; (ii) `agent1`'s own log shows it executing the step until its cancel poller interrupted it **4.938 s after** the reap; (iii) its 16 detached claim pollers were mid-long-poll and re-upserted on schedule 5.011 s later. Nothing was lost, stale, or partitioned.
- **Expected:** `docs/high-availability.md:393-395` documents the `LEFT JOIN` as covering "the rarer case where the agent row was already deleted first", explicitly framed as `DeleteStaleAgents` (5 min threshold) having acted — i.e. the row is gone *because the agent was already dead for five minutes*. The doc's own framing at `:373-377` is that the heartbeat exists precisely so "a busy-but-alive agent that wasn't polling for new claims" is not "misidentified as dead and deleted". Deregistration by a duplicate-ID sibling produces the same row state with none of the liveness meaning, and — as W2-3 established and this run re-confirms — the heartbeat cannot undo it. `grep -rn -i deregister docs/` still matches nothing outside `docs/superpowers/`, so the deregistration behaviour itself remains undocumented (W2-1's carry-forward note, unchanged).
- **Notes:**
  - **What is new here, beyond W2-3 — and two of these claims were overstated in an earlier version, so read the boundary carefully.** W2-3 had **already** identified `UpsertAgentOnClaim` as the sole healer (its Notes at `FINDINGS.md:704-705`) and had **already** measured an absence window, at **≤28.642 s** (its Arm C2b). Neither the mechanism nor the existence of a bounded window is a W2-7 discovery. What is genuinely new: (1) **the trigger is injection-free** — an ordinary `docker compose stop` of a duplicate-ID process, no `DELETE`, no psql, no fault of any kind; (2) **the healer's grid structure and periodicity are measured exactly** rather than inferred — 6 clusters of exactly 17 requests, within-cluster spread 0.005-0.017 s, period 30.033-30.040 s, dropping to 16 while a run occupies the normal slot; (3) **a second, tighter measurement of the absence window** taken from the Postgres statement log rather than from a ~1.6 s poll — **28.434 s** exact-to-the-millisecond against W2-3's poll-bounded ≤28.642 s, and the two agree; (4) it was phase-locked on the **first** aimed attempt, so the path is not merely reachable but *predictable* from two grids either measurement can obtain in two minutes.
  - **Carry-forward that qualifies W2-2's restart fact.** W2-2 established that a bare `docker compose restart` gives ~1.013 s before SIGKILL. That was measured on an agent **holding a run**, whose drain is unbounded. On an **idle** agent the SIGTERM path completes in milliseconds and the `Deregister` always lands: `DELETE` at **0.373 s / 0.367 s / 0.301 s** after the host-clock SIGTERM in three statement-log measurements (`w2-7/partD-deletes.txt`, `w2-7/partD-attempt1.txt`), plus **0.319 s** from the agent's own `"agent deregistered"` line in Part A2. (These four straddle two clocks — host for the `docker` command, DB for the statement log — and the session's skew could not be resolved below ~0.3 s, because the skew probe itself runs through `docker exec`; treat them as ~0.3-0.4 s, not to the millisecond.) **So `restart` of an idle duplicate deletes the shared row too** — the window is just short because `Register` follows ~0.6 s later, and the detector caught exactly one `agents=0` sample for it at `02:39:15.452`. Any later task that needs a `DELETE` should stop an *idle* agent; any task that needs the row *kept* must not restart one.
  - **The 16-vs-17 upsert count is a usable side channel**: the cluster size drops by exactly the number of busy execution slots, because a slot executing a run is not issuing claim requests.

## W2-7 — observation: nothing anywhere reveals that two processes share one agent ID — one `agents` row, one `last_seen_at`, no warning, no metric, and the inventory API shows a single healthy agent throughout
- **Invariant:** none violated. Filed on operational risk: this is the property that makes every entry above hard to diagnose rather than a defect in itself.
- **Severity:** minor
- **Repro:** same scenario; `w2-7/gate-agents.json`, `w2-7/partC.txt` (final inventory read), `w2-7/detector.txt`, `w2-7/controllers-full.log`.
- **Observed:**
  - `GET /api/v1/agents` returned **exactly one** row for `agent1` at the start of the session (`hostname 3a533c2b4d1a`, `capabilities ["native","container"]`) and **exactly one** at the end (`hostname 7dae999b8d0e`) — the hostname is simply whichever process registered most recently. Two processes, one row, no duplicate, no flag.
  - The reason is structural: `UpsertAgent` is `ON CONFLICT (id) DO UPDATE` with `hostname/os/labels/version/capabilities/env` all set from `EXCLUDED` (`postgres.go:1083-1108`), and `UpsertAgentOnClaim` is the same shape with a non-destructive variant of the clause (`:1118-1142`). The `agents` table has no session id, generation or fencing column (`internal/store/migrations/001_init.up.sql:18-27`), so there is nothing a second registration could collide with.
  - `last_seen_at` is refreshed by **both** processes, so the one liveness signal an operator has looks *healthier* with a duplicate present, not worse: sampled every ~1.6 s across Part B it never exceeded 13.21 s (`w2-7/detector.txt`) — two 15 s tickers interleaving.
  - **Zero log lines** across three controllers name a duplicate, a re-registration conflict, or a hostname change: `w2-7/controllers-full.log` (493 KB, whole session) contains only the two `"agent process replaced"` warnings, and those name the *victim run*, not the collision.
  - The hostname *does* change in the DB on every registration, so a duplicate on a different host is in principle detectable by watching `agents.hostname` flap — but nothing surfaces or alerts on it, and a duplicate on the *same* host would not flap at all.
- **Expected:** nothing promises detection, which is why this is an observation. `docs/agents.md:41-48` is the closest text and it is about *distinct* IDs sharing a host. The gap is that the agent ID is treated as an identity everywhere and as a lock nowhere.
- **Notes:** cheapest diagnosability fix, no schema change: have `handleAgentRegister` log at Warn when it upserts over a row whose `last_seen_at` is younger than the heartbeat interval (15 s) — a genuine restart's predecessor is either gone or seconds stale, whereas a concurrent twin is always sub-15-s fresh. That is a one-line heuristic; it would have fired at `02:37:51.863`, `02:39:15.829`, `02:48:23.419` and `02:55:5x` in this session, and it gives an operator something to alert on while the fencing token is designed.

## W2-7 — observation: the zombie window on a *connected* agent is **0.939-4.938 s, not W1-5's 40.2-162.5 s** — a 5 s cancel poller stopped local execution after every one of six terminal writes. The plan's "same zombie shape as W1-5" is right in kind and wrong by one to two orders of magnitude
- **Invariant:** I6 (zombie containment) — "after the controller fails a run, observed agent-side behavior is *measured and documented* (not pass/fail: the architecture has no hard fencing; the operator judges acceptability)" (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:53`). By its own wording this produces a measurement, not a verdict.
- **Severity:** minor
- **Repro:** same scenario, all parts. Evidence: `w2-7/agent1-full.log`, `w2-7/agent1b-full.log`, `w2-7/partA1.txt`, `w2-7/partA2.txt`, `w2-7/partB.txt`, `w2-7/partC.txt`, `w2-7/partD-attempt1.txt`.
- **Observed:**
  - **Six terminal writes, six soft fences, every one under 5 s** (terminal `updated_at` → the executing process's own `"master reported run terminal; interrupting run"` line):

    | Run | Killer | Terminal write | Fence | Latency |
    |---|---|---|---|---|
    | `e05842ef` | twin startup reconcile | `02:37:51.867` | `02:37:53.665` | **1.798 s** |
    | `7b7ffb9d` | twin startup reconcile | `02:39:15.833` | `02:39:16.796` | **0.963 s** |
    | `33a02f4a` | twin heartbeat reconcile | `02:41:15.854` | `02:41:17.880` | **2.026 s** |
    | `0384cca0` | `agent1` heartbeat reconcile | `02:41:17.536` | `02:41:20.928` | **3.392 s** |
    | `1f0ba2a3` | stuck-run reaper | `02:53:36.545` | `02:53:41.483` | **4.938 s** |
    | `7d7e4ad5` | third client's `finish` (`Succeeded`) | `02:56:47.627` | `02:56:48.566` | **0.939 s** |

    The last is included because it shows the fence fires on `Succeeded` as well as `Failed`.
  - **The mechanism** is `RunClaim`'s cancel poller (`internal/agent/orchestrator.go:124-152`): a goroutine that `GetRun`s every `CancelPollInterval = 5 s` (`:37`) and calls `cancelRun()` as soon as `isTerminalRunStatus` is true (`:22-29`), distinguishing `Cancelled` (`cancelledByMaster`) from any other terminal status (`reapedByMaster`, `:141-145`). Every measured latency is ≤ one 5 s tick, as it must be. The step then dies with `"step exec error ... context canceled"` and the process logs `"run already terminal at master; skipping local outputs/finish"` ~2 s later.
  - **Consequence for I2, and the reason this observation matters more than it looks.** `edge-sideeffect` appends one line per second to the shared `/data/sideeffect.log`. Its run was failed at `02:41:15.854` and its executor fenced at `02:41:17.880`; the file's last two lines are `run,69,02:41:16` and `run,70,02:41:17` — **exactly 2 further side effects**, 70 lines total for a run that would have written 120 (`w2-7/sideeffect.log`). So the post-terminal side-effect exposure of a duplicate-ID reap is bounded by the poll interval, not open-ended. **Instrument disclosure:** the `/data` bind is shared across every agent container, so this *is* the shared-volume append-only log I2 prescribes — but no arm here produced two *executions* of one step, so I2 is not violated and is cited only for the boundedness measurement.
  - **Why W1-5 saw 40.2 s and 162.5 s instead:** its zombie was produced by a one-way *partition*, so the poller's `GetRun` could not reach the controller and simply logged `"cancel poller: get run failed"` (`orchestrator.go:136`) and continued. A reachable controller is the whole difference. Incidentally the poller is one of the few agent calls that survives token expiry, because it is a **GET** and `Client.do` replays a 401 only for GETs (`internal/agent/client.go:93-96`).
- **Expected:** nothing in `docs/` promises a fence, and the campaign spec's own I6 says "the architecture has no hard fencing" — which remains true: this is a *soft*, cooperative fence that any loss of connectivity removes. It is nonetheless materially better than "no fencing at all". **What the W2 plan (`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-30-edge-case-campaign-w2.md:228`) and the Task 8 brief got wrong is a magnitude, not the phenomenon — stated precisely, because the earlier framing of this entry overclaimed.** Part A's literal deliverable ("confirm the run is `Failed` while `agent1`'s process is **still executing** the step") **did hold**: the run went `Failed` at `02:37:51.867` and `agent1` was still executing until `02:37:53.665`, a genuine **1.798 s** of terminal-but-executing. What is false is the implication carried by "the same zombie shape as W1-5" — that the exposure is open-ended, as W1-5's 40.2 s and 162.5 s were. On a connected agent it is bounded by one `CancelPollInterval`, so an arm designed to *watch* a zombie accumulate side effects has ~5 s of signal, not minutes, and must be instrumented at second resolution or it will see nothing. Recorded as a correction per the campaign rule that a contradicted "verified fact" is itself a finding.
- **Notes:** for any later wave: an unfenced-zombie claim requires showing the agent could **not** reach `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}`. On a healthy network the exposure after a wrongful terminal write is ≤ `CancelPollInterval` (5 s) plus the step's own write cadence, and the work already done is lost either way — which is why the Part A/B harm is *destroyed work*, not *duplicated side effects*.

## W2-7 — observation: reaping a mutex-holding run releases the mutex 2.026 s before its holder stops executing. I3's letter is satisfied; the release is premature, not leaked
- **Invariant:** **I3 (no lock leaks)** — "mutex/semaphore/concurrency slots are released when the holder reaches a terminal state (verified by a successor run acquiring the lock AND by direct inspection of `mutex_holders` / `named_lock_slots`)" (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:50`). **Held, in both of its clauses.** The risk runs the other way, so this is explicitly *not* an I3 violation.
- **Severity:** minor
- **Repro:** same scenario, Part B step 4. Evidence: `w2-7/partB.txt`, `w2-7/sideeffect.log`, `w2-7/partC.txt` (final lock census).
- **Observed:**
  - `33a02f4a` (`edge-sideeffect`, `spec.concurrency.mutex: edge-mutex`) held the mutex from `02:40:07.867`; `mutex_holders` sampled `1` at every 5 s sample through `02:41:15.737`, then `0` from the first sample after the reap (`02:41:21.310`). `MarkRunFinished` releases both `mutex_holders` and `named_lock_slots` in the run's own transaction (`postgres.go:757-777`), inside `failOrphanedRun`.
  - The holder process kept executing for **2.026 s** past that release (fence at `02:41:17.880`) and appended 2 more lines to the shared side-effect log in that window.
  - A successor `edge-mutex-successor` triggered afterwards was claimed at `02:43:16.046` and `Succeeded` at `02:43:16.066`, with `acquired-mutex-ok` in its log — I3's successor clause, satisfied.
  - Final census after the whole scenario: `mutex_holders=0 named_lock_slots=0`.
- **Expected:** `docs/high-availability.md:397-399` states the release is deliberate and explains why it must be per-run ("`MarkRunFinished` also releases the run's `mutex_holders` and `named_lock_slots` rows; a bulk update would leak those locks and block unrelated runs indefinitely"). So the eager release **is** the documented design, and the overlap is a consequence of it, not a contradiction of it.
- **Notes:** W1-5 already filed the same shape with a far larger window (its observation: the reaper released the mutex ~40.2 s before the unfenced agent stopped, successor acquired in 732 ms). This is an independent second measurement with the window bounded by the 5 s cancel poller instead of by a partition — **2.026 s rather than 40.2 s** — which is the useful addition: on a connected agent the mutex-overlap exposure is bounded by `CancelPollInterval`, and only a partition makes it unbounded. Cross-referenced, not re-filed. Any real fix has to make the *agent* relinquish before the controller releases, which the current one-way (controller-writes, agent-polls) protocol cannot express. **Not demonstrated here:** no successor was triggered *inside* the 2.026 s window, so the concurrent-overlap consequence is derived from the window's existence plus the successor's later 20 ms acquisition, not observed directly.

## W2-7 — resolved branch-internal asset bug: the campaign's Postgres-instrumentation snippet puts two `ALTER SYSTEM` statements in one `psql -c`, which Postgres refuses — and the failure is *silent*, so an armed-looking instrument records nothing

- **Classification:** branch-internal asset defect, fixed on this branch. Reported outside both the violation and observation tallies, per the W1 checkpoint's third bucket (`FINDINGS.md:480`, tally rule at `:487`). Filed rather than omitted **because** no product code is implicated — that is the bucket's entry criterion, not a reason to leave it out; the three precedents are W1-4's `spec.mutex` payload bug (`FINDINGS.md:278-279`), W2-3's own `inject.sh nginx-unblock` bug (`:769-770`) and W2-6's schedule-endpoint bug (`:1107-1109`). With this entry W2's third-bucket count is **3**. *(An earlier version of this sentence said "**2**, not 1" and listed only two precedents — it overlooked W2-3's entry, which carries its own `Classification:` line and is unambiguously in the same bucket. Corrected at the W2 checkpoint while tallying; the campaign total across W1 and W2 is 4.)*
- **Severity:** n/a (campaign asset)
- **Repro:** `docker compose … exec -T postgres psql -U unified -c "ALTER SYSTEM SET log_statement='all'; ALTER SYSTEM SET log_line_prefix='%m [%p] h=%h ';" -c "SELECT pg_reload_conf();"` → `ERROR: ALTER SYSTEM cannot run inside a transaction block`, then `t`, then `log_statement = none` in a fresh session. Found while arming the same instrument for W2-7 Part D, whose entire aiming procedure depends on it.
- **Observed:** two statements in a single `-c` are sent as one implicit transaction, and `ALTER SYSTEM` cannot run inside one, so **both** SETs are rejected. The failure mode is what makes this worth an entry rather than a typo fix: `pg_reload_conf()` in the following `-c` still returns `t`, `SHOW` in the *same* session still reports whatever that session inherited, and the only way to notice is `SHOW log_statement` in a **new** session. An armed-looking instrument therefore records nothing, and any scenario that keys a measurement on the statement log would report a clean negative result it never actually measured. The correct form is **one `-c` per `ALTER SYSTEM`**, which is what `w2-3`, `w2-4` and the W2-7 runbook already use.
- **Expected:** the snippet is a checked-in campaign asset, and **an earlier version of this line said "only its W2-6 copy carried the broken form", which was wrong and understated the blast radius by two runbooks.** The broken form was carried by **six committed copies across three runbooks**: W2-6's gate G1 and its teardown (the two this line originally named), **W2-1's arm and revert** (`scenarios/w2-1-leader-exclusion.md:209`, `:535` as committed at `2a1dcf5`) and **W2-3's arm and revert** (`scenarios/w2-3-reaper-boundary.md:430-432`, `:527` at the same commit). All six are corrected on this branch: W2-6's two when this entry was first written, and W2-1's and W2-3's four in `6dd3608` (`test(edgecase): split combined ALTER SYSTEM instrument in W2-1 and W2-3 runbooks`), each now one `ALTER SYSTEM` per `-c` with the silent-failure mode written into the surrounding comment so the next author does not re-collapse the calls. Two textual occurrences of the combined form survive **deliberately** and must not be "fixed": W2-3's teardown caveat (`w2-3-reaper-boundary.md:635-637`) quotes it as the thing being warned about, and W2-6's execution note (`w2-6-schedule-duplicate-fire.md:729-731`) quotes it as that session's text of record — which is itself the checked-in-text-versus-executed drift this entry documents.
- **Notes:**
  - **This invalidated no W2-6 measurement, and nobody should later fear those numbers.** The defect is a drift between W2-6's checked-in runbook text and what W2-6 actually executed — the execution armed the instrument successfully. Its own report records `log_statement='all'` **in force** at `00:40:46.7` (with `pg_locks` showing one scheduler-lock holder in the same capture) and the `ALTER SYSTEM RESET` verified in a fresh session at `01:29:13`; and W2-6's baseline finding that a *new* session reads `log_statement = none` is itself independent proof the revert landed. All 48 statement-logged fires, the 1-3 ms window and the `last_fired_at` bind-parameter analysis stand unchanged.
  - The same "verify in a session that did not issue the reload" rule applies to **arming**, not just reverting. W2-6 had already noted the same-session caveat for the revert; the arm has it too, and that is the only reason this went unnoticed for a wave.

## W2-8 — the approval endpoint never looks at the run, so an ordinary `unified-cli approve` writes `Approved` and a **named human** onto a run that has already failed, and the audit log records it as a `204` success — reproduced 5 times; the exposure window is the gate's whole remaining `timeoutMinutes` (documented default **60 minutes**), not the ~60 s reaper period, demonstrated on a **`Cancelled`** run that accepted the decision 9.19 s after the cancel with **9m48s** still on `timeout_at`
- **Invariant:** **I7 (state display consistency)** — "run status, approval status, and audit rows never contradict each other or reality" (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:54`). This is the literal fit and it is violated on **all three** of I7's named surfaces at once, in one single-clock read: `runs.status='Failed'`, `run_approvals.status='Approved'` with `decided_by='env:UNIFIED_TOKEN'`, and an `audit_logs` row `action=run.approval.decide, resource=<that run id>, status=204`. **I1 (`...:48`) is NOT violated and must not be claimed** — checked, not assumed: after the decision the run re-read `Failed` with `updated_at` unchanged, `step_reports` step 2 (`after`) stayed `Skipped`, and `SELECT count(*) FROM runs WHERE triggered_by LIKE '%<runID>%'` returned **0** (`w2-8/partA-contradiction.txt`). The run reaches exactly one terminal state and that state is *correct* — the gate really did fail. What is false is the *decision record*, which is I7's subject, not I1's. **NOT I5:** no fault is injected anywhere in this entry; the window is produced by ordinary API use. **Scope note added after Part F — read the sibling entry directly below before reusing any of the negative claims above.** Everything in this entry was measured with the decision landing *after* the agent was already gone or already knew (timeout path: the agent had exited; Part D: +9.19 s, past its cancel-detection fence). Part F fired the same decision **inside** that fence and the picture is different there: the post-gate step body **executes**. So "nothing executes" and "no side effect fired" hold for *this* entry's trials and must not be quoted as general properties of a post-terminal approve.
- **Severity:** major
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-8-approval-race.md`, Part A (trials A-api and A-cli) and Part D. Stack = plain `test/ha`, no overlay, both agents up. Evidence: `w2-8/gate.txt`, `w2-8/gate-g5-throwaway.txt`, `w2-8/partA-api.txt`, `w2-8/partA-cli.txt`, `w2-8/partA-contradiction.txt`, `w2-8/partA-cli-evidence.txt`, `w2-8/partA-audit.txt`, `w2-8/partA-agent-log.txt`, `w2-8/partB-controller-http.txt`, `w2-8/partD.txt`, `w2-8/partD2.txt`, `w2-8/partD2-ui.txt`, `w2-8/partB2-and-operator-surface.txt`, `w2-8/partE.txt`, `w2-8/reaper-latency.txt`, `w2-8/teardown.txt`.
- **Observed:**
  - **The vulnerable state arises by itself and was caught on a sampler line, not inferred afterwards.** Run `2ccb87ac-72f5-4b1d-a92e-11893d7737d4` (`edge-approval-short`, claimed by `agent1`): `timeout_at = 03:58:15.674054`, the agent logged `"approval timed out"` at `03:58:15.690801463`, and the run went `Failed` at `runs.updated_at = 03:58:15.698289`. The 1 s DB sampler's line at `db=03:58:16.360629` reads `Failed|…|Pending|-` — **run terminal, approval row still `Pending`, `decided_by` NULL** (`w2-8/partA-api.txt`, SAMPLE 47). No injection, no outage, no race: per `internal/agent/approval.go:17-20` the agent has no decision endpoint, so it never touches the row.
  - **One POST, `204`, and the contradiction sits in one read.** `POST /api/v1/runs/2ccb87ac…/approvals/1 {"decision":"approve","comment":"w2-8 A-api"}` → **`204` in 7.712 ms**. The single `UNION ALL` read (`w2-8/partA-contradiction.txt`, one DB clock, one statement) returns `RUN … Failed … updated_at 03:58:15.698289` and `APPR … Approved … decided_at 03:58:16.729928 … decided_by env:UNIFIED_TOKEN … timeout_at 03:58:15.674054` — the decision is recorded **1.031639 s after the run's own terminal write** and **1.055874 s after the deadline it was supposed to obey**.
  - **The audit row is the strongest artifact, and it is written synchronously as documented.** `GET /api/v1/audit` returned, with no polling, `{"id":9,"occurredAt":"2026-07-30T03:58:16.731969Z","actor":"env:UNIFIED_TOKEN","method":"POST","path":"/api/v1/runs/2ccb87ac…/approvals/1","action":"run.approval.decide","resource":"2ccb87ac…","status":204}` (`w2-8/partA-audit.txt`). **The route IS audited — verified live, not assumed.** W2-7 established that agent identity routes carry no audit middleware; this is a human route registered at `internal/controller/server.go:383` under the subrouter that mounts `auditLogMiddleware` at `:357`, and the row's presence proves it.
  - **The trigger is a documented CLI command that reports success.** Trial A-cli, run `607815a7-60a4-4943-8ca1-dbebc66b8c93`: with the run already `Failed` at `04:03:15.072418`, `unified-cli approve 607815a7… 1 --comment "w2-8 cli"` printed **`approved step 1 of run 607815a7-60a4-4943-8ca1-dbebc66b8c93`** and exited **rc=0** (`w2-8/partA-cli.txt`). `internal/cli/approvals.go:31-68` performs no run-state or approval-state lookup and treats any status < 400 as success. The row became `Approved` / `env:UNIFIED_TOKEN` / comment `w2-8 cli` at `04:03:15.674123`, **601.705 ms after the terminal write** (`w2-8/partA-cli-evidence.txt`).
  - **Part D — the window is NOT bounded by the ~60 s reaper period, and no timeout is required at all.** The reaper's guard needs `timeout_at < now()` (`internal/store/postgres.go:2473-2484`), so a gate that has not yet expired is untouchable by it. Run `5c28b436-409a-4492-8c65-9fe04e995e6c` (`edge-approval`, `timeoutMinutes: 10`) reached `WaitingApproval` at `04:34:42.460`, was **cancelled** (`POST /runs/{id}/cancel` → 204) at `04:34:42.484`, and read `Cancelled` at `updated_at 04:34:42.537675` with the approval row `Pending` and `timeout_at 04:44:39.618499` — **`expires_in = 00:09:48.175639`**. The approve POST at `04:34:51.687` returned **`204`** and wrote `Approved` / `env:UNIFIED_TOKEN` at `04:34:51.732555`, **9.19488 s after the cancel** (`w2-8/partD.txt`). So the exposure window is the **remaining `timeoutMinutes`**, whose documented default is **60 minutes** (`docs/jobs.md:1732`, `internal/agent/approval.go:22`), not the ≤60 s the timeout path gives.
  - **The audit log for that run reads as a cancel followed by an approval of the same run.** Three rows, one resource: `run.trigger 200 @04:34:39.16765`, `run.cancel 204 @04:34:42.540545`, `run.approval.decide` **`204`** `@04:34:51.734672` (`w2-8/partD.txt`). Nothing in that trail indicates the third row had no effect.
  - **Part D2 — nothing ever reconciles a `Pending` row on a terminal run before `timeout_at`.** Run `75831861-74cd-40de-be26-76e335697548`, cancelled at `04:35:27`, sampled every ~5.6 s for **185.0 s** (`04:35:27.204 → 04:38:32.093`, 34 samples) across **3** reaper sweeps (`04:36:05`, `04:37:05`, `04:38:05` — grid in `w2-8/partC-grid-pre.txt`): every sample reads `Cancelled|appr=Pending|step1=WaitingApproval` (`w2-8/partD2.txt`). There is no cancel-path or finish-path reconciliation of `run_approvals` anywhere.
  - **The falsified state is displayed to operators on every surface, and one surface actively invites it.** Code-read, then confirmed against the live API bodies:
    - `unified-cli run approvals 2ccb87ac…` prints `[1] gate  Approved  by env:UNIFIED_TOKEN at 2026-07-30 12:58:16` while `unified-cli run show` for the same run prints `Status: Failed` and `[1] gate  Failed (exit 0)` (`w2-8/partB2-and-operator-surface.txt`). Two commands, two contradictory answers, neither flagging the other. (The CLI renders local time, `+09:00` of the UTC values used throughout this entry.)
    - Web UI: `web/src/routes/RunDetail.svelte:1316-1320` renders `Decided by <strong>{approval0.decidedBy}</strong>` (text at `:1318`) under any step that is not `WaitingApproval`, so the `Failed` gate step is captioned "Decided by env:UNIFIED_TOKEN" with **no** indication the decision post-dated the failure.
    - **For the Part D shape the UI is not merely a display, it is the trigger.** `GET /api/v1/runs/75831861…/steps` returns step 1 `"status":"WaitingApproval"` on a run whose own status is `Cancelled` (`w2-8/partD2-ui.txt`), and `RunDetail.svelte` renders the **Approve / Reject buttons** on exactly that condition. **Cite the branch that actually renders for this fixture:** the template has the whole approval panel duplicated, once for a parallel group (`{#if group.steps.length > 1}` at `:1206`, panel `:1247-1266`, caption `:1269-1273`) and once for a single sequential step (`{:else}` "Single sequential step" at `:1276`, panel **`:1295-1315`** with the two `decideApproval` handlers at **`:1308`** / **`:1312`**, caption `:1316-1320`). `edge-approval` puts one step per stage — `partD2-ui.txt` shows `stageIndex` 0/1/2 — so `group.steps.length == 1` and it is the **single-sequential** copy that draws the live buttons. The condition (`s0.status === 'WaitingApproval'`) is identical in both copies, so the finding is unaffected by which one renders; an earlier version of this entry cited only the grouped/matrix branch (`:1246-1266`/`:1268-1273`), which is the copy this fixture never reaches. `unified-cli run show` prints the same: `Status: Cancelled` with `[1] gate WaitingApproval` and `[2] after Pending`. A UI-only operator with `dev` rights can therefore reach this state by clicking a live button on a cancelled run.
  - **Reproduced 5 times on `Failed` runs and once on a `Cancelled` run.** The final pre-teardown census (`w2-8/teardown.txt`) groups every approval row the scenario produced: `Failed | Approved | env:UNIFIED_TOKEN | 5`, `Cancelled | Approved | env:UNIFIED_TOKEN | 1`, against `Failed | TimedOut | system | 11` and `Succeeded | Approved | env:UNIFIED_TOKEN | 1`. The six offending rows, with `decided_at − runs.updated_at`: `2ccb87ac` **+1.031639 s**, `607815a7` **+0.601705 s**, `a9b519eb` **+19.054933 s**, `4159a2c8` **+19.168898 s**, `5ae3b964` **+19.85074 s**, `5c28b436` **+9.19488 s**. (The three ~19 s cases are Part C attempts C5/C6/C8 — the identical defect, reached while aiming at the reaper; they are corroboration for this entry, not separate findings.)
  - **Controls establish that `204` is a real discrimination and not this endpoint's only answer** (`w2-8/partE.txt`): E1 approve **while genuinely `WaitingApproval`** → `204`, run continued to `Succeeded`, all three steps `Succeeded` (so the fixture and the happy path work); E2 second approve on that row → **`409` "already decided"**, `decided_at` unchanged; E3 approve step index `0`, which has no approval row → **`404` "no pending approval"**; E4 approve an already-`TimedOut` row → **`409`**, row untouched. All four are audited with their own status — `audit_logs` holds 204/409/404 rows for `run.approval.decide` (`w2-8/partE.txt`) — so the audit trail's `status` column *does* distinguish the effective write from the rejected one, which is what makes the `204` rows above unambiguous rather than arguable.
- **Expected:** **The docs do not prohibit a post-terminal decision — `grep -rn -i "timeout_at|after the timeout|expired approval" docs/*.md` returns one unrelated line (`docs/high-availability.md:85`) — so on the "is it forbidden" limb the docs are silent, not contradicted, and the violation rests on I7.** Three readings of the one relevant passage, `docs/jobs.md:1739-1742`, distinguished deliberately because W2-7 was corrected for citing a passage that *sanctioned* the behaviour it was cited against:
  1. **Not a prohibition.** "When the step times out, the agent fails the step itself, so the run is correctly marked as Failed.
