- **Severity:** **minor** — a storage leak with no correctness consequence for any run, whose in-product producer needs two failures in a row (below). Argued against this file's bands (`:6-8`) rather than defaulted.
- **Observed:**
  - **The state was produced by hand and then observed through a real run** (`w3-1/partC-payload-orphan.txt`). `mc rm` of **only** the `.meta` at `06:57:05.103Z`, leaving the 16 MiB payload alone in the bucket. Run `c733d2f5` (agent2, `06:57:06`) then reported **`CACHE-PRESENT complete=yes` / `entries=257` / `SHORT-FILES=0`** and the agent logged `INFO "cache hit"`. **The interposer's record of that run's RESTORE is a single request** — `GET …tar.zst 200 bytes=16909275` (`w3-1/partC-payload-orphan.txt:33`) — **with no `.meta` GET at all**, which is the narrow claim this entry needs and is the wording `:2009` already uses for the sibling measurement. **It is NOT "a single request, and no `.meta` request of any kind", as an earlier version of this bullet said:** the same capture shows **four** requests for run `c733d2f5` (`:33-36`) — the restore `GET` plus the deferred save's three-request multipart write — and `:37-38` records that save re-creating the `.meta`, which is exactly what the bullet at `:1995` goes on to say.
  - **That is the strongest form of the fact, and it corrects the plan.** The plan (`:59`) says a payload without `.meta` is "invisible to both `findBestMatch` and `DeleteExpired`", which is true (`cache.go:188` and `:232` both `continue` on non-`.meta` keys). But it is **not** invisible to `Restore`: the exact-key branch is `store.Get(oKey + ".tar.zst")` at `cache.go:142` and reads no metadata at all. So the orphan is not dead storage — **it is live, authoritative, un-expirable cache content.**
  - **Nothing in the tree can reclaim it, and the enumeration is W3-6's, cross-referenced rather than re-derived.** `FINDINGS.md:1836` already enumerated every object-store `List` call site: `internal/cache/cache.go:182` (`caches/`), `:223` (`jobPrefix`), `internal/controller/api_artifacts.go:127`, `internal/controller/log_trim.go:76`, `internal/controller/run_retention.go:148` — **and both cache-side ones skip non-`.meta` keys**, while the other three are keyed by a run id. There is no orphan reconciler and no bucket-wide sweep anywhere in the tree.
  - **The one thing that CAN reclaim it, stated because it bounds the finding honestly:** a future `Save` under the *identical* key overwrites the payload and writes a fresh `.meta`, after which the entry is collectable again — which is exactly what happened at `06:57:07` in this very capture. **So "leaks forever" is precisely true only for a key that never recurs — and the documented key idiom is content-hashed** (`docs/jobs.md:1381`: `key: go-vendor-{{ hashFile "go.sum" }}` — `:1382` is the `restoreKeys:` line, which an earlier version of this entry cited by mistake), so a key that never recurs is the *normal* case, not the exotic one.
  - **The in-product producer is code-read, not reproduced live, and is labelled as such** (the W2-3 Arm D precedent). `Save` writes the archive first and the `.meta` second (`cache.go:82`, `:98`); on a `.meta` put failure it compensates with a best-effort `Delete` of the archive and **names this exact hazard in its own comment** (`:99-102`: "without its `.meta` it is invisible to both lookup and GC (which iterate `.meta` only), so it would leak forever"). If that compensating `Delete` also fails it logs `slog.Warn` and returns (`:103-105`). **So the product's own producer is a `.meta` `Put` failure followed by a `Delete` failure — two object-store errors in a row, which is precisely what an object-store outage looks like.** The comment is cited as evidence of intent only: an inline comment inside a function body is **not** a contract under this branch's rule.
- **Expected:** **contradicted, not silent.** `docs/jobs.md:1407`, the closing sentence of the Cache section, read in full and in context (`:1401-1407`) before citing, per the W2-7 lesson:

  > "`ttlDays` is capped at 365 (jobs asking for a longer TTL fail validation at parse time) so a single entry cannot pin itself, and the storage it occupies, indefinitely."

  The sentence asserts a property of the system — that no single entry pins its storage indefinitely — and a payload orphan does exactly that: no sweep can see it, no lookup can expire it, and per the bullets above the exact-key read path keeps serving it. **The cap is real and works; the guarantee the cap is offered as evidence for does not hold in this state.** `docs/high-availability.md:83` ("Only the leader deletes expired cache entries") is an arbitration row and is deliberately **not** cited as a limb — it says nothing about completeness. The full survey (counts **97 / 33 / 87 / 11**, untruncated, `w3-1/partE-docs-survey.txt`) turns up no other statement about cache storage bounds.
- **Notes:** the fix is `Save`'s own comment taken one step further, since the compensating `Delete` being best-effort *is* the producer. Three options: **retry the compensating `Delete`**; or **invert the two `Put`s** (write a `.meta` first, archive second) so the failure mode becomes the benign orphan of the deletion-order note below; or **give the cleanup sweep a second pass** that lists `caches/` and deletes any `.tar.zst` with no sibling `.meta` — the last is ~10 lines, needs no ordering change, and would also collect the out-of-band case this entry produced. **Counter-argument to the third:** a reconciler that deletes objects with no sibling is a footgun if it ever observes a legitimate concurrent `Save` between its two `Put`s, so it needs an age floor — the same shape `run_retention.go`'s "race (b)" re-sweep already uses.

### W3-1 (observation): `ttlDays` bounds nothing on the restore path — an entry whose `.meta` carried a **hand-planted** `expiresAt` of `2020-01-01T00:00:00Z`, over six years past, was restored in full, because the exact-key restore never reads `.meta`

- **Scenario:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w3-1-cache-delete-vs-restore.md` Part C3. Same session and stack.
- **Invariant:** **none contradicted** (see the first entry's I4 argument). Filed as an observation on design, with the docs weighed below and found **silent on this specific point**.
- **Severity:** **minor (observation)**.
- **Observed:** the live `.meta` was replaced with one differing **only** in `expiresAt` — `{"originalKey":"edge-cache-torn-v1","expiresAt":"2020-01-01T00:00:00Z","size":16909275,"ownerJob":"edge-cache-torn"}`, 116 bytes, planted at `06:58:09` and read back from the store to confirm (`w3-1/partC-expired-meta.txt`). Run `1991a82f` (agent1, `06:58:11`) then logged `INFO "cache hit"` and reported **`CACHE-PRESENT complete=yes` / `entries=257` / `SHORT-FILES=0`**, restoring an entry **six and a half years expired**. The interposer's record of that run is again a **single** cache request — `GET …tar.zst 200 bytes=16909275` — with **no `.meta` GET at all**.
  - **`ExpiresAt` is read in exactly two functions and neither is on this path — *CODE-READ*, and labelled as such because this scenario archived no code-read capture** (see `:2022`; the sibling completeness claim at `:2017` carries the same label, and this one was re-verified against the source at review): `DeleteExpired` (`cache.go:202`, the 24 h sweeper — see the entry below) and `findBestMatch` (`:244`, the `restoreKeys` **fallback** path only, which reads the field a second time at `:252` when it ranks candidates). The exact-key branch (`:141-149`) consults no metadata whatsoever.
  - **One attempt is recorded as VOID rather than hidden.** An earlier try at `06:57:33` used `sed` inside the `mc` container, which has no `sed`; the rewrite produced an empty body, `mc pipe` refused it, and the `.meta` was never modified — so run `ef359444`'s hit was on a *live* entry and proves nothing. The capture carries that banner immediately before the real attempt.
- **Expected:** **silent, not contradicted.** The full survey (counts **97 / 33 / 87 / 11**, untruncated, `w3-1/partE-docs-survey.txt`) contains no statement that an expired entry stops being *served*. The only expiry statements are `docs/jobs.md:1384`'s inline `ttlDays: 30 # cache expiry (default: 30 days)` inside a YAML example and `docs/high-availability.md:83`'s "Only the leader deletes expired cache entries", both about **deletion** — which is what the sweeper does. The behaviour is consistent with a literal reading: `ttlDays` schedules a deletion; it does not gate a read.
- **Notes:** the composition is what makes this worth recording rather than shrugging at, and it composes three separately-defensible decisions: (1) the read path never checks expiry; (2) the only thing that does check is a sweeper on a hardcoded 24 h ticker whose **first** fire is at t+24 h (entry below); (3) a run that hits the entry re-saves it and refreshes `expiresAt` (`orchestrator.go:984-1001`, unconditional — measured at `06:53:06` in the first entry's Notes). **Together these mean a `ttlDays: 1` entry is not observably a one-day entry in any sense an operator can act on.** Cheapest fix, one line: have `Restore`'s exact-key branch read the sibling `.meta` and treat an expired entry as a miss, which is what the fallback path already does at `:244`. **Counter-argument:** it doubles the object-store round trips on the hot path (a `.meta` GET per restore) for a property the sweeper is supposed to provide, and it makes a restore fail closed during a partial outage where the `.meta` is unreachable but the payload is not.

### W3-1 (observation): `RunCacheCleanup` is the only background loop in the controller with no interval parameter — 24 h hardcoded inline, no flag, no env, no config, and `time.NewTicker` means the first fire is at t+24 h

- **Scenario:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w3-1-cache-delete-vs-restore.md` §"The scheduler lever does not exist". **Code-read plus one measured absence.**
- **Invariant:** none. Filed on design and testability.
- **Severity:** **minor (observation)**.
- **Observed:**
  - `internal/controller/scheduler.go:219` — `func RunCacheCleanup(ctx context.Context, st store.Store, obj objectstore.ObjectStore)` — and `:223` — `ticker := time.NewTicker(24 * time.Hour)`. The call site passes nothing (`cmd/controller/main.go:401`).
  - **The enumeration was pasted from a terminal — and that terminal produced NO CAPTURE, which is a weaker standing than an earlier version of this bullet claimed** (it invoked the W3-6 lesson, "pasted rather than asserted", as if the paste were archived). **`w3-1/` contains no code-read capture whatsoever**: its only `grep` captures are `partE-docs-survey.txt` (a `docs/` survey) and `partE-already-ruled.txt` (greps over `FINDINGS.md` and `test/edgecase/`); neither greps the Go tree. **The enumeration below is nonetheless exactly right** — all twelve `func Run*` line numbers were re-verified against the source at review — but this half of the entry rests on **unarchived terminal output**, so a re-runner should re-derive it rather than cite it, and the same caveat governs `:2010` and the lever grep below. Every `func Run*(ctx …)` in `internal/controller/`, twelve of them: `RunApprovalReaper` (`approval_reaper.go:21`, `interval`), `RunAppSourceReconciler` (`appsource_reconciler.go:56`, `tick`), `RunAppSourceSyncReaper` (`appsource_sync_reaper.go:24`, `interval, staleAfter`), `RunLogArchiver` (`archiver.go:19`, `interval`), `RunAuditRetention` (`audit_retention.go:21`, `interval`), `RunLogTrim` (`log_trim.go:28`, `interval`), `RunQueuedRunReaper` (`queuedrun_reaper.go:29`, `interval, minAge, staleAfter`), `RunRunRetention` (`run_retention.go:26`, `interval`), `RunScheduler` (`scheduler.go:23`, `tick`), `RunGitResolver` (`scheduler.go:262`, `tick, deadline`), `RunStuckRunReaper` (`stuckrun_reaper.go:22`, `interval, staleAfter, grace`) — **and `RunCacheCleanup`, which takes none.** Eleven of twelve take one; the exception is this one. **Read that as a statement about the *signature*, not about configurability, because the difference is narrower than it sounds and the entry rests only on the narrow part.** Every one of those eleven call sites passes a **literal** as well (`cmd/controller/main.go:367`, `:400`, `:403`, `:404`, `:410`, `:414`, `:420`, `:426`, `:436`, `:444`, `:448` — `200*time.Millisecond`, `30*time.Second`, `time.Minute`, `time.Hour`), so **none of the other eleven intervals is operator-configurable either**, and nothing here should be read as "the other eleven are". What the parameter buys is testability and a one-line path to a flag: **for the eleven the knob exists and is merely never turned; here there is no knob**, so both cost a signature change.
  - **There is no lever of any kind.** `grep -rn "CACHE_CLEANUP\|cache-cleanup\|cacheCleanupInterval\|CacheCleanup"` over the tree, excluding this campaign's own files, returns **10** hits and not one is a flag, an env var or a config field: the call site (`cmd/controller/main.go:401`), the doc comment and the function (`scheduler.go:216`, `:219`), the leader helper (`:231`, `:235`), one docs row (`docs/high-availability.md:83`) and four unit-test lines (`scheduler_test.go:52`, `:56`, `:59`, `:63` — two tests, each contributing its name and its call, both of which only assert the nil-store early return). **The "**7**" an earlier version of this bullet stated is wrong and was never captured:** re-running the entry's own pattern at this branch's HEAD returns **10**, and the bullet's own prose already enumerated **8** line references while calling it 7. Per the bullet above, this is unarchived terminal output. **The conclusion is unaffected and stands: not one of the 10 is a flag, an env var or a config field.**
  - **The measured half, with the scope word narrowed to what the capture can carry:** from boot (`06:44:24Z`) to the **census stamp** (`07:01:18.759Z`) — ~16.9 minutes, and **not** the whole session, which ran on to `07:02:54.124Z` (`w3-1/teardown.txt:1`, `:50`; the last ~95 s held only the arm-clear and `down -v`) — `grep -ci "cache cleanup"` and `grep -ci "expired cache"` over all three controllers' complete logs each return **0** (`w3-1/census.txt`, whose own heading says "the WHOLE session"; it is the stamp that bounds the zero, not the heading — same correction as `:1940`). **That is an absence over a window this runbook ended, not a "never"** — but it is the point: because `time.NewTicker` does not fire on creation, **no rig that lives less than 24 h can observe this job at all**, and every scenario in this campaign is torn down in minutes.
  - **Cross-reference, not a re-filing, per this branch's convention (`:798`, modelled by W3-5 at `:1936`).** `FINDINGS.md:1837` is this branch's only prior ruling on a hardcoded sweeper cadence: W3-6 **skipped its Part D** because run retention's interval is a hardcoded `time.Hour` **at the call site** (`cmd/controller/main.go:426`) and `time.NewTicker`'s first fire is an hour out, so the sweeper could not be observed on a minutes-long rig and everything about it stayed code-read. **That is the same consequence from a different cause**, and the difference is the whole reason this is a separate entry rather than a second instance of W3-6's: there the parameter **exists** and the call site merely passes a literal (so a test can drive the loop and a one-line call-site change makes it configurable); here the parameter **does not exist**. W3-6 already owns the "no rig shorter than the interval can observe the job" lesson; this entry adds the stronger case, the twelve-way enumeration and the zero-lever grep, and does not re-file W3-6's.
- **Expected:** **silent, not contradicted.** `docs/high-availability.md:83` is the only doc line about this job and says only "Only the leader deletes expired cache entries" — an arbitration statement with no cadence in it. The full survey (counts **97 / 33 / 87 / 11**, `w3-1/partE-docs-survey.txt`) contains no cache-cleanup cadence or configuration anywhere. W2-1's null-result entry (`FINDINGS.md:577-582`) already established that this job's advisory lock (`0x63616368`) recorded **zero** acquisition attempts on a rig with no object store, for the different reason that the goroutine was never started; **this entry is the other half of that instruction** — the goroutine now *is* started, and the lock is still never taken, because the ticker has not fired.
- **Notes:** two consequences. **For operators:** an object store filling with expired cache entries can only be swept once a day, on a schedule that resets to t+24 h on every controller restart — so a cluster that redeploys daily may sweep its cache **never**, and there is no way to change that without a code change. **For this campaign:** any future scenario that needs `DeleteExpired` to actually execute must either add the parameter (a product change, out of scope) or drive `cache.DeleteExpired` directly from an `edgeprobe` build-tagged test — the pattern W0-2 established, and the one the W2 checkpoint already recommended for audit retention (`FINDINGS.md:1503`) for exactly this reason. **Cheapest fix: give it the interval parameter the other eleven have** (a one-line signature change and a one-line call site), and separately consider an immediate first sweep at start-up, which is what would make the job observable at all.
- **Deletion order, recorded here rather than as a separate entry because it is a null result and a good one.** `DeleteExpired` deletes the **archive first** (`cache.go:204`) and the `.meta` **second** (`:207`), with `continue` on either failure and no transaction. An interruption between them therefore leaves the **benign** orphan — `.meta` with no payload, which Part C2 measured as a clean `cache miss` — and can never produce the **leaking** payload-with-no-`.meta` orphan of the entry above. **The order is the safe one.** Whether it is deliberate is arguable and the argument is worth stating: `:202-211` carries no comment justifying it, but `Save` carries an explicit one for the mirror-image case (`:99-102`, quoted in the entry above) and compensates accordingly — **a codebase that reasons about exactly this hazard in `Save` and then orders `DeleteExpired`'s two deletes the matching way is most naturally read as deliberate-but-undocumented.** **Code-read: `DeleteExpired` never executed on this rig** (see the measured absence above), so both end-states were produced by hand (Parts C1/C2) rather than by an interrupted sweep.
- **Part C2's clean miss was attributed to a code path, not assumed, and it corrects the plan on a third point.** The plan (`:59`) explains the benign orphan as "expired by construction → `findBestMatch` skips it (`cache.go:244`)". That reasoning holds **only** for an orphan left by `DeleteExpired`, which by definition only touches already-expired entries. The orphan Part C2 actually produced had a **live** `expiresAt` (`2026-08-01T06:53:06Z`), so `findBestMatch` selected it and the miss came from one line further on. **The interposer's access log shows the exact four-request shape at `06:56:20`, in order** (`w3-1/partC-meta-orphan.txt`): `404 GET …tar.zst` (the exact-key `Restore`, `cache.go:142`) → `200 GET /unified-cd-cache/` (`findBestMatch`'s `List(jobPrefix)`, `:223`, 657 B) → `200 GET …meta` (the candidate read, `:234`, 126 B) → `404 GET …tar.zst` again (the fallback `Get`, `:162`). The miss is `ErrCacheMiss` from `:162-167`, **not** the expiry skip at `:244`. Same clean outcome, different path — and only one of the two is what an interrupted sweeper would produce.

### W3-1 (campaign asset, fixed on this branch): `inject.sh s3-slow` emitted `limit_rate`, which nginx ignores under the interposer's `proxy_buffering off`, so the verb was a silent no-op

- **Classification:** **branch-internal campaign-asset defect, found and fixed inside this branch — neither a violation nor an observation**, and reported outside both tallies per this file's rule (`FINDINGS.md:481-483`, the W1-4 precedent).
- **Scenario:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w3-1-cache-delete-vs-restore.md` Part A1 (arm confirmation). **The defect was caught by the runbook's own probe-confirmation step** — the W2-5 rule this wave has now been paid back for three times.
- **Observed:** `inject.sh s3-slow 262144` wrote `limit_rate 262144;` into the interposer's arm include. The arm loaded correctly — `s3-show` listed the file, `s3-probe` returned `X-S3-Arm: none+slow[262144B/s]`, and the proxy stamped `arm=none+slow[262144B/s]` on the request line itself — **and it throttled nothing**: a **10 s bounded read** through the armed proxy returned the **whole** 16909275-byte object — byte-identical to the same bounded read taken direct to Garage as a control — against an armed rate that should have yielded ~2.6 MB in that window (`w3-1/partA-arm.txt:33-36`, the capture's own expectation printed at `:36`). **The `rt=0.060` an earlier version of this bullet presented as a pasted access-log field is NOT one, and is dropped rather than re-cited:** its only occurrence in the entire wave is `partA-arm.txt:38`, which is the capture's own DIAGNOSIS **prose**, and no `s3proxy` access-log line anywhere carries it — exactly the class `:2097` polices ("if a block looks like pasted output, it must be pasted output"). The conclusion is independently and fully carried by the bounded-read pair. **Two campaign assets still quote the withdrawn figure and are OUT OF SCOPE of the commit that made this correction** — `test/edgecase/tools/inject.sh:182` and `test/edgecase/compose/nginx-s3.conf:135`, both in explanatory comments; `test/edgecase/README.md:149` **has** been corrected. Neither comment's conclusion changes, and a follow-up should restate them on `partA-arm.txt:33-36` the way this bullet now does. **So this was not a reload that failed to take** — the W2-5 trap — and the capture rules that out per-request before diagnosing anything else.
  - **Cause:** `nginx-s3.conf` sets `proxy_buffering off` at server level, deliberately (its header explains why: buffering a GET would collapse the very cache-restore stream W3-1 needs). nginx does not apply `limit_rate` to an unbuffered upstream response.
  - **Fix, and it is the semantically better knob anyway:** the verb now emits `proxy_buffering on; proxy_limit_rate <rate>;` at **location** level, leaving the server-level `off` as the default for the unarmed path and every other arm (so W3-6's artifact-PUT timing is untouched). `proxy_limit_rate` throttles nginx's read **from Garage**, so the upstream GET is genuinely in flight for the whole window instead of having already drained into nginx's buffers — which is exactly what a delete-vs-in-flight-GET measurement requires. **Verified after the change, through the verb**: 2703126 bytes in a 10 s bounded read = 270312 B/s against an armed 262144 B/s, versus the full 16909275 bytes direct to Garage in the same 10 s (`w3-1/partA-arm.txt`).
  - Fixed in `test/edgecase/tools/inject.sh`, with the measurement recorded in the verb's own comment and cross-referenced from `test/edgecase/compose/nginx-s3.conf` (beside the `proxy_buffering off` that causes it) and from `test/edgecase/README.md`'s verb table.
- **Notes:** **the lesson is not "s3-slow was broken" but that an arm can be verifiably LOADED and still be a no-op.** Every check the tooling already had — `s3-show`, `s3-probe`, and the `arm=` stamp on every access-log line — confirms *which config the serving worker held*, and all three passed while the arm did nothing. **Only a probe that measures the arm's intended EFFECT catches this class**, and for a rate arm that costs one bounded read.

  **THE RULE THIS YIELDS, RESTATED — and W4, W5 and W6 all inherit this tooling, so the restatement matters more than the bug did.** The rule is **"a verb is verified when SOME capture measures its effect"**, *not* "when its comment carries a measurement". **An earlier version of this bullet used the comment test and mis-enumerated the table in both directions**, calling `s3-block`'s non-`ANY` forms unverified and omitting the one verb that genuinely shares this failure class. Corrected, arm by arm:
  - **`s3-block` is the table's BEST-verified arm, not an unverified one.** It performs a per-arm effect probe **at arm time**, parameterised on the actual method and prefix — `s3_probe "$meth" "/$pfx"` plus a `GET /_control_never_matched_` control that must **not** be blocked (`inject.sh:139-140`) — and W3-2 confirmed it live and prefix-scoped: blocked probe **403**, control **200**, two further prefix probes **200** (`FINDINGS.md:1799`).
  - **`steplock` is the arm that actually shares `s3-slow`'s failure class, and its own comment admits it** (`inject.sh:86-87`: it "is a **no-op** against `nginx-edge.conf`, so check the response code after arming") — a silent no-op whenever the wrong nginx conf is mounted, which is identical in shape to `limit_rate` under `proxy_buffering off`. **It is effect-confirmed, but by EXECUTION rather than by its comment**: W2-5's probe-confirmed **403** at arm time and the three agent-originated 403s in the nginx log (`FINDINGS.md:895`, `:929`).
  - **`s3-latency`** is the one verb whose comment does carry its own measurement (`inject.sh:150-156`, W3-2's `9.702 s` recapture).

  **So nothing in the verb table is unverified today.** The standing requirement is forward-looking: **every NEW or CHANGED arm must ship with an effect measurement** — a capture, a probe the verb itself prints, or the comment, in that order of preference — and **"the file is present, `s3-show` lists it, `nginx -t` passed and the `arm=` stamp appears" is never one of them.**

## Checkpoint: W3 complete
- **Scenarios run: all six, none dropped — plus a seventh set of entries from the infrastructure build-out that had to precede them.** W3-1 (cache object deleted / torn mid-restore — Parts A1 / A' / B1 / B2 / C1 / C2 / C3 plus the cleanup-interval read: **5 entries**), W3-2 (S3 outage during archival and artifact upload — Parts A / A2 / B arms 1-2 / C / D across two stack phases: **4 entries**), W3-3 (mixed-KEK replicas — Parts A / B / B5 / C / D / D3: **4 entries**), W3-4 (bulk log-append duplication — Parts A / B / C / D / D2 / D3 / D4, plus a Part E archive limb re-run added after Task 3 gave the rig an object store: **2 entries**), W3-5 (archive seal racing the final log flush — Parts A / B / C / E: **2 entries**), W3-6 (run retention racing an in-flight artifact upload — Parts A / B / C, with Part D skipped and the skip argued in the entry: **3 entries**), and **W3-infra** — Task 3's rig build-out, which is not a scenario and produced **3 entries** of its own plus the object store every later scenario depended on. **23 entries total = 9 violation entries (9 new violations) + 13 observations + 1 resolved branch-internal asset bug.** *(Tallied mechanically from the title markers and Severity lines, then reconciled by hand. Several classifications and attributions were corrected during this wave's reviews and the corrections are load-bearing — the numbers below are as the entries now state them and must not be re-merged.)* **The 9/13 split supersedes an 8/14 one that stood until the branch review: W3-2's archiver-backoff entry (`:1736`) was filed as an observation ruling "the docs are silent" on `docs/operations.md:53`, a published paragraph that names the log archiver and whose two clauses that entry's own measurements falsify. It is now a documented-contract violation. The entry count is unchanged — one entry moved buckets, nothing was added or split.**
- **Classification rule:** unchanged from W0, W1 and W2 and applied to every entry above — **violation** = observed behaviour contradicts an invariant (I1-I7) **or a documented contract**; **observation** = behaviour matched expectations but reveals a risk; **third bucket** = a defect in the campaign's own assets, fixed inside this branch, reported outside both tallies. The W1/W2 clarifications carry unchanged: the "documented contract" limb requires a *published* promise (an exported API field, a schema column, or a statement in `docs/`) and **not** an unexported helper's own doc comment. **W3 extended that limb once, and the extension is deliberate: an inline comment inside the body of an *exported, routed* handler is also not a contract.** W3-6 (`:1838`) met the letter of the W2 rule — `handleArtifactUpload` is exported and routed, so the rule as written did not disqualify its comment — and declined to use it anyway, on the ground that a comment inside a function body is not *published* to anyone. The same call is made at W3-1 (`:1996`) for `Save`'s comment and at W3-2 (`:1790`) for the archiver's. **In all three the comment is cited as evidence of intent, which is worth more than a contract would be, because in two of the three the comment's own second sentence is false in exactly the case its first sentence warns about.** W2's *procedural* amendment (an entry may be a violation entry without being an additive violation) had no occasion to fire this wave: all nine violation entries are additive, so **violation entries 9 = new violations 9**. **One number replaces it and W4 must carry it forward: of those 9, exactly one — W3-6 (`:1864`) — was NEVER EXECUTED.** It is a code read filed as a violation because leaving it unfiled would have made a sibling entry's completeness claim false, and it says so in its own Repro line ("**NONE. This entry was not executed**"). It is counted in the tally, and any triage that acts on it must measure it first; the entry itself specifies the measurement and says it takes minutes.
- **Violations: 9 new — 4 major, 5 minor.** Per scenario: W3-1 **1** (0 major / 1 minor), W3-2 **2** (0/2), W3-3 **2** (1/1), W3-4 **1** (1/0), W3-5 **1** (1/0), W3-6 **2** (1/1), W3-infra **0**. By primary limb: **documented contract 5, I4 2, I5 1, I7 1** — which is the same 9, and is the first wave in the campaign where the *contract* limb outnumbers every invariant — and, after the branch review moved `:1736`, outnumbers **all four invariant limbs combined** (5 versus 4) and is the outright majority of the wave's violations.
  - **major, I4 — W3-4** (`:1521`): a bulk log append is a bare per-line loop with no transaction and no idempotency, so any failure the agent sees for a request the controller already committed is answered by re-sending it. A `Succeeded` run whose workload emitted **2002** lines was stored, and served, with **8493** — **4.24x** — with 2000 duplicate groups and 8490 of 8491 burst lines out of position; the cheaper arm turned one 200 ms proxy-timeout **arm** into three separately timed-out requests each leaving a committed prefix (**173/193/190**, surplus 556 = 2558 rows against 2002 emitted). Postgres confirms there was never a transaction to roll back: **2558** `INSERT INTO logs`, **0** `rollback`. Three of I4's four clauses breached; the fourth (`archives stay readable`) was code-read at filing and **is now measured** — the AMENDED bullet at `:1557` re-ran it against the Garage rig and the archiver applies no de-duplication and no ordering repair, exactly as the code read predicted, with the severity unchanged as that bullet predicted.
  - **major, documented contract (`docs/secrets.md:420`) — W3-3** (`:1620`): the sole HA row of the Secrets Guide's Troubleshooting table prescribes, unconditionally, "Give every replica the identical key file" as the fix for "`decrypt` errors in HA setup". **Applied verbatim it left 9 of 9 runs failing on all three replicas**, and after the repair the row's stated *cause* is false as well. Nothing anywhere carries key identity — **67 ciphertext bytes fully accounted for** (`1+6+12+32+16`) with no room for one, six table columns, `LocalKeyManager` is `struct{ kek []byte }`, no startup check, `/readyz` 200 — and no surface can enumerate the damage. **THE REMEDY CONVERTS A RECOVERABLE STATE INTO PERMANENT LOSS, and that is the sentence to triage on:** the blob written during the mixed window was **readable** (phase 2, 6/6 Succeeded on the replica holding the wrong key) until the prescribed repair removed that key, after which 9/9 fail on all three. The doc fix is therefore an *ordering* fix before it is a wording fix — retrieve or re-`secret set` the affected secrets before removing the wrong key file, or retain that file until you have — and that fix depends on no code change. **Critical is stated as arguable and the escalation is left explicitly to the operator.**
  - **major, documented contract (`docs/cli.md:164`) — W3-6** (`:1864`): `DELETE /api/v1/jobs/{name}` deletes the `jobs` row and nothing else, so every artifact and every log archive of every run of the job survives the delete **unconditionally, with no race and no window**, while the doc promises "Deletes a job and all its run history (steps, logs, artifacts)". Strictly worse than its sibling on every axis — no window, both key spaces, every run, and an automated caller (the AppSource prune path). **NOT EXECUTED — code read only**, and that is the single most important thing about it.
  - **major, I4 at a restated scope — W3-5** (`:1878`): log lines are accepted with a `204` and a zero-byte body, discarded by the archive seal, and the loss is invisible to the sender and to every surface of the run. Part B hit on **attempt 1 of a cap of 3** with nothing synthetic in it: **12 stored + 78 discarded = 90 lines offered**, the 78 measured controller-side as nine `slog.Warn` lines summing to 78 and independently derived from a 78.014 s span at one tick per second — 87 % of the run's output, with the sender's whole experience of it being **one INFO line**. **The entry does not stretch I4's text**; it restates the finding at the scope both arms demonstrate (*a run terminalised by the controller while the agent still holds a log backlog permanently and silently loses that backlog*), which strictly contains I4's literal case, and it says plainly that the literal `Succeeded`-with-a-real-workload case may be unreachable in production rather than merely unproduced.
  - **minor, documented contract (`docs/jobs.md:1407`) — W3-1** (`:1986`): a cache payload whose `.meta` is missing is invisible to both `.meta`-iterating consumers, has no expiry any code can read, and is **still served** on every exact-key restore — because `Restore`'s exact-key branch is a bare `store.Get(oKey + ".tar.zst")` that reads no metadata at all. The contradicted sentence is the Cache section's closing guarantee that the `ttlDays` cap exists "so a single entry cannot pin itself, and the storage it occupies, indefinitely". **This corrects the plan** (`plan:59`), which had the orphan as dead storage; it is live, authoritative, un-expirable cache content.
  - **W3-2 contributes TWO violations, both minor, and they are listed in one bullet because they are two limbs of one Part A / Part D session rather than two findings that were merged.** **(1) minor, documented contract (`docs/operations.md:53`) — W3-2** (`:1736`): §"Sweep failure backoff" names the log archiver first and makes two promises about it — that the backoff state "is held by the current leader only and resets on failover (each problem candidate is retried once, then re-excluded)", and that broken runs "can no longer starve archival … for everything newer". **Both are false under measurement.** `runArchiveAsLeader` releases the advisory lock inside each pass, so leadership rotates every tick with no fault at all, every replica keeps its own ladder, and the two poisoned runs drew **20 attempts in 14.7 minutes** against the 4 a single ladder gives — the state resets every *tick*, not on failover. And exclusion from the candidate set does not buy exclusion from the **cluster-wide** archiver lock, which a failing pass holds for its whole duration: 55 of 70 samples granted over a ~110 s window, one hold **≥ 17.46 s** continuous, ~44 s per failing `Put`, *derived* ~15 minutes for the batch limit of 20 — during which no replica can archive a healthy run either. **This entry was filed as an observation ruling the docs silent and was reclassified at branch review**; `:1498` had handed it the paragraph by name and `:547`, `:575`, `:1490` and `:2078` were already treating it as the contract limb for this exact shape. Severity argued at minor, not defaulted, and the escalation boundary is named in the entry. **(2) minor, I5 — W3-2** (`:1710`): a controller that restarts while the object store is unreachable exits(1) in ~4 s with no retry and does not come back on its own — 12 samples over 2 minutes after the store was restored, all `exited exit=1 restartCount=0` — while the **same condition is tolerated indefinitely by an already-running replica** (7 m 30.8 s, `/readyz` 200 throughout, a run triggered mid-outage `Succeeded` in 34.9 s). The HA guide documents this crash-on-start for Vault (`:322-325`) and not for S3 — **and the "S3 is optional" framing an earlier version of this bullet used is withdrawn**: `docs/high-availability.md:310` says "S3 is required for HA operation", which contradicts `docs/configuration.md:68`'s Required: **No** for the same variable and makes fail-closed-at-start a defensible design rather than an absurd one. What is left is that the behaviour is stated nowhere, the two references disagree, and the *running* replica does not fail closed at all. Filed at minor for consistency with W0-1's identical shape against Postgres, and the entry reconciles in full why the *same commit* reads I5 as violated here and not violated in the companion entry at `:1736` (which is now a violation on a *contract* limb, not on I5 — the I5 reconciliation between the two is unaffected by the reclassification, because `:1736` still rules I5 not violated for want of any documented archival-latency bound).
  - **minor, I7 — W3-3** (`:1643`): a run that fails before its first step report displays that step as `Pending` forever on a `Failed` run — **23 of 23**, permanent. **Reached by this scenario, not caused by it**; the class is "runs that terminate before their first step report", and it is **not** closed by PR #113, which fixed the stale-`Running` shape on a different path.
  - **minor, documented contract (`docs/configuration.md:84`, `docs/operations.md:47`) — W3-6** (`:1813`): `deleteRunEverywhere` re-sweeps the log-archive key after `DeleteRun` to catch a write landing in its own deletion window and has **no equivalent re-sweep of `artifacts/<runID>/`**. Hit on **attempt 1 of a cap of 6**, on a positive in-flight signal rather than a sleep: a 32 MiB artifact survived the deletion of its run, still downloadable in full. Part C then produced the asymmetry directly — **the same execution of the same function caught the late log archive and missed the late artifact**.
- **Not counted in either tally — 1 resolved branch-internal asset bug** (W1 had 1, W2 had 3, so the campaign total is now **5**). **W3-1** (`:2031`): `inject.sh s3-slow` emitted `limit_rate`, which nginx ignores under the interposer's server-level `proxy_buffering off`, so the verb was a **silent no-op** — a 10 s bounded read through the armed proxy returned the whole 16909275-byte object, byte-identical to the direct-to-Garage control, against an armed rate that should have yielded ~2.6 MB (`w3-1/partA-arm.txt:33-36`). *(The `rt=0.060` an earlier version of this line quoted as an access-log field is the capture's own DIAGNOSIS prose at `partA-arm.txt:38`; see `:2035`.)* **The lesson is not "s3-slow was broken".** Every check the tooling already had — `s3-show`, `s3-probe`, and the `arm=` stamp on every access-log line — confirms *which config the serving worker held*, and all three passed while the arm did nothing. See the tooling note below; **this is the wave's most transferable rule and W4, W5 and W6 all inherit the tooling it applies to.**
  - **A second asset defect was found and is deliberately NOT in that count, and the reason is worth stating because it is the boundary of the third bucket.** The W3-2 **runbook itself** shipped a wrong advisory-lock constant — `logArchiverLockKey = 0x6C6F6761` is **1819240289**, and the runbook said `1819242081` — which voided two captures (`w3-2/gate-g5-leader.txt` and `w3-2/partA-lockhold.txt`, both of which read as clean 0-of-60 and 0-of-75 negatives and are now VOID-bannered in the capture itself). It is recorded in the runbook rather than filed as a third-bucket entry because it was caught and corrected **inside the same session, before any finding rested on it**, and it never left that runbook. **The follow-on lesson is the transferable part and it repeats a W2-6 shape exactly: striking a constant in a fact table is not enough — the wrong literal initially survived the correction in two operative steps, so `grep` the whole file for the digits.** Note this is the *third* time the campaign has been bitten by an advisory-lock constant (W2-6's `1702389604`, W3-2's `1819242081`): **check any lock key against `SELECT objid FROM pg_locks WHERE locktype='advisory'` rather than trusting a hex-to-decimal conversion.**
- **Observations: 13 — 1 major, 12 minor.** By scenario: **W3-1 (3)**, **W3-2 (2)**, **W3-3 (2)**, **W3-4 (1)**, **W3-5 (1)**, **W3-6 (1)**, **W3-infra (3)**. Twelve carry `Severity: minor (observation)` and say "observation" in the title, per `:481`. *(Was 14 — 1 major, 13 minor, with W3-2 at 3 — until the branch review moved `:1736` into the violation tally; see the note at `:2049`.)*
  - **THE ONE THAT IS NOT MINOR, AND IT IS A STRUCTURAL FIRST FOR THIS FILE — read this before reading the tally as a typo. W3-1 (`:1938`) is the file's first `major`-severity observation.** A cache restore torn mid-stream leaves the workspace half-populated; the step is reported **`Succeeded`** (the cache path is lenient by explicit written policy, which **corrects the plan's** prediction that it would `markFailed`); the next step reads the debris **9.7 ms later** (*derived*: `INSPECT-START 06:59:09.873Z` minus the agent's `WARN` at `06:59:09.863310565Z` = **9.689 ms**, and the `INSPECT-START` stamp is ms-precision, so the true value lies in **9.689–10.689 ms**) and, because `deps/` is non-empty, does not regenerate; and the deferred save then overwrites the good 16 MiB cache entry with the 1.9 MiB debris — **permanently, with no fault still present**, self-perpetuating across agents (two further completely unfaulted runs on a *different* agent restored the same 30 files with the same truncated `f0030` and both reported `Succeeded`), with the whole operator-visible surface being **one `WARN` line in one agent container's log**. **It was reclassified from violation to observation at review, and the reclassification is the point.** The entry disclaims both of `:478`'s violation limbs *by its own upheld rulings*: I4 is not contradicted clause by clause (its subject is "Log/**artifact** integrity" and a cache entry is neither, on W3-5's binding narrow reading of the same clause), and the docs are ruled **silent, not contradicted** on a survey re-derived independently in review. Calling it a violation would have been exactly the stretch `:1509` forbids, in the opposite direction from the four stretches that rule was written for. **Severity and classification are orthogonal axes** — the bands at `:6-8` measure impact, the violation/observation split records whether a **codified** promise was broken — and this entry is the case that proves they must be. **What the label costs is stated here as it is in the entry: an observation is easier to skip and this one should not be skipped. Adding a cache-integrity clause to the invariant set would make it a violation with no new evidence.**
  - **W3-1's other two:** `ttlDays` bounds nothing on the restore path — an entry **over six years past its `expiresAt`** was restored in full (**the six years is a hand-planted literal, `expiresAt: 2020-01-01T00:00:00Z`, not an aged entry**; the interval is accurate — 2403 days = 6.580 years, so "over six years" is right and `:2009`'s "six and a half" is a conservative round-down — and it is disclosed here and in the entry's title because those are the two places a triager reads first), because the exact-key branch never reads `.meta`, and the composition with a 24 h-first-fire sweeper and an unconditional re-save on every hit means a `ttlDays: 1` entry is not observably a one-day entry in any sense an operator can act on; and `RunCacheCleanup` is **the only one of the controller's twelve `Run*` background loops with no interval parameter at all** — 24 h hardcoded inline, zero levers in a repo-wide grep, and `time.NewTicker` means the first fire is at t+24 h, so **no rig that lives less than 24 h can observe the job**. The entry is careful that eleven-of-twelve is a statement about the *signature*, not about configurability: every one of the other eleven call sites passes a literal too.
  - **W3-2's two — and the third that used to be listed here is now a violation, `:1736`, counted in the tally above and not here:** the archiver's object/record compensation works exactly as its comment claims, but the compensating delete is best-effort **against the store that just failed**, and when it fails the orphan is unreachable by every code path in the tree — plus, separately and independently actionable, **the archiver's `Put` body is a non-rewindable `bytes.Buffer`, so minio-go's retry is dead on this path** and every hung upload reports a `ContentLength` mismatch that names no outage (one-line fix: `bytes.NewReader`); and a blocked artifact upload fails the step and the run exactly as it should and then **no surface anywhere names the cause** — zero controller `WARN`/`ERROR`, zero lines in the run's own log, and a failed step reporting `"exitCode":0`.
  - **W3-3's two:** the mixed-KEK blast radius is exactly the documented one and nothing else — **8 of 24** secret-using runs failed, every fetch attributed **per request** to the replica that served it (3/13/8, so the real result is "controller3 failed 8 of the 8 it served and the other two 0 of 16", and reporting 8/24 as a measured 33 % rate would overstate what the number is), with **no LB retry and no upstream ejection** because `http_500` is absent from `proxy_next_upstream` — *the exact opposite of W3-4's experience with the same `max_fails=1` config, and the difference is the status code, not the rig*; and 23 decrypt failures produced **zero** controller `ERROR` lines while the agent logged 23 `ERROR`s that say nothing — the wrong-key case is the only one of three decrypt classes that stays at `Warn`, **the one message that names the fix is the one that cannot fire**, and the agent discards the 500 body so the run's own log reads only `http 500: response omitted`.
  - **W3-4's, W3-5's and W3-6's one apiece:** the duplication is completely silent and **the drop marker cannot fire for this class by construction** — it is a *loss* marker and there is no *surplus* marker and no notion of one — while `logs/stats` reports the inflated count as authoritative and nothing anywhere records what the workload emitted, so the corruption is undetectable from inside the system; a bulk log-append spanning two sealed runs counts every drop and attributes them all to whichever run is **last in the body** (`droppedRun` is a scalar, overwritten in the loop), latent today because every one of the **nine** `AppendLogBulk` call sites in the tree supplies a single run id, and live the moment any sender puts more than one run in a body; and **five of seven run-scoped API surfaces answer 200 for a run that `GET /runs/{id}` answers 404 for**, two of them serving the deleted run's data — the orphaned 32 MiB artifact is listable *and* downloadable in full with any agent credential, while the `PUT` that created it is the one run-scoped write in the API with **no audit row at all**.
  - **W3-infra's three, none of which is a scenario:** the reference HA load-balancer configuration inherits nginx's **1 MiB default body cap**, so every artifact upload over 1 MiB dies at the LB with a **413** and no user-facing doc anywhere names the knob (`grep` over `docs/*.md` → **0** hits) — **filed first as a major contract violation and withdrawn at review**, because this branch had *already ruled the other way on the same sentence* at `:1687`, and filing the broad reading would have made the branch say two incompatible things about `docs/high-availability.md:253-254`; an agent with the `docker` CLI but no reachable daemon advertises the `container` capability, so the scheduler reports a container job as schedulable and the run fails at claim time instead; and artifact objects are stored under a `.tar.gz` key but contain zstd, with four user-facing doc passages describing the format correctly while the key does not.
- **I4 WAS THIS WAVE'S WHOLE PURPOSE, AND THE GAP IS CLOSED — but the honest form of that answer names which scenarios produced I4 evidence and which did not.** The W2 checkpoint closed with "**I4 recorded zero violations because W2 never exercised it** … that is a coverage gap owned by W3, not a pass." It is now exercised on both sides. **I4 breached, twice:** W3-4 (`:1521`) breaches three of I4's four clauses on its literal text — a `Succeeded` run, a count that does not match, duplicates, and reordering — and is the only entry in the campaign to do so; W3-5 (`:1878`) is filed on I4 at a **restated scope** that strictly contains I4's literal case, having argued the clause as a three-way conjunct that neither of its arms satisfies in full and having refused to stretch the text to close the gap. **I4 confirmed to hold, with measurements rather than assertions:** W3-2's archiver entry (`:1736` — reclassified at branch review to a documented-contract violation; its I4 ruling is unchanged, because I4 was never the limb it turned on) held I4 on every clause it reaches and produced the positive control the wave needed — `line_count` **30 = 30** `logs` rows on all six phase-1 runs, every object present, one archive read back end to end through `mc cat` as three well-formed ndjson records; W3-2's compensation observation (`:1760`) attacked the "archives stay readable" clause directly and confirmed the code's claim that the breaking state is not constructible, **and established the ruling the rest of the wave inherits — an object with no record is an orphan, not an unreadable archive**; W3-2's Part C (`:1793`) checked the one that would have mattered (a `Succeeded` run with a missing artifact) and got a `Failed` run with a correctly-absent artifact; W3-6 (`:1813`) inherited that ruling rather than re-arguing it. **I4 NOT exercised, and the entry says so:** W3-3 (`:1580`) — "its scope clause is 'a Succeeded run's', and the failing runs emit nothing", on a rig that at the time had no object store. **Two entries had to argue I4's clauses individually and one ruled it *not* contradicted:** W3-5 (above) and **W3-1 (`:1945`), which ruled I4 NOT contradicted, clause by clause, in its own Invariant line rather than burying it** — every emitted line was stored, nothing was reordered, and the cache archive it left behind is a perfectly well-formed readable 1966149-byte tar+zstd that is simply the wrong contents. **So: the gap is closed, I4 is the wave's most-cited invariant, and the one thing W4+ must not do is read W3-1's or W3-6's "I4 not contradicted" as I4 passing — in both cases I4's *subject* does not reach the object that was corrupted.** Which is the next bullet.
- **THE WAVE FOUND TWO HOLES IN THE CAMPAIGN'S OWN INVARIANT SET, AND THIS IS THE CHECKPOINT'S MOST IMPORTANT CARRY-FORWARD. They are coverage gaps, not absences of defect.** Both were found the same way — an entry with a real, permanent, silent data defect looking for the invariant that covers it and finding none — and both are flagged at the entries themselves (`:1621`, `:1947`) with an explicit instruction to raise them here.
  1. **No secret-store integrity clause (W3-3).** I4's subject is "Log/**artifact** integrity" by its own text (`…design.md:51`) and nothing covers secrets. I7 (`:54`) enumerates "run status, approval status, and audit rows", so the secrets list API reporting a **permanently undecryptable** secret as present is adjacent to that and outside its stated scope, and stretching it would repeat the mis-attribution W2-9 had withdrawn in review. **W3-3 escaped the gap only because it found a published-contract limb** (`docs/secrets.md:420`), and that limb is an accident of the docs, not of the invariant set.
  2. **No cache-integrity clause (W3-1).** Same reason, same text. The other six invariants are about run terminality (I1), at-most-once execution (I2), lock leaks (I3), bounded recovery (I5), zombie windows (I6) and status/audit consistency (I7). **W3-1 had no contract limb** — its Expected rules the docs silent on a survey re-derived independently in review — so here the gap is **load-bearing**: it is the sole reason the wave's most damaging cache finding is filed as an observation rather than a violation.
  - **What should be done about it, stated as a recommendation rather than an action, because amending the spec is out of this wave's scope.** The cheapest correct fix is a **single generalised clause**, not two: I4's subject should read *stored-state integrity* and enumerate the object classes the system persists on a run's behalf — logs, artifacts, log archives, **cache entries** and **secrets** — with the existing predicate ("matches what was written; no duplicates, no reordering; stays readable and decryptable") applied to each. Two narrow clauses would leave the next class (`run_outputs`? `step_outputs`? the git-template cache?) uncovered by the identical argument. **Whoever takes it must re-run the classification of exactly two entries and no others** — W3-1 (`:1938`) becomes a violation with no new evidence, and W3-3 (`:1620`) gains a second limb without changing its disposition — and must **not** disturb W3-1 (`:1986`), which already stands on a contract. **Until that happens, W4-W6 must not read "no invariant contradicted" as "nothing is wrong": check whether the invariant's *subject* reaches the object before concluding anything from its silence.** This is the third consecutive wave in which the campaign's own definitions, rather than the product, were the thing that needed correcting — W1 refined I1-vs-I7, W2 amended it again and added the non-additive-violation rule, and W3 found two holes in the coverage itself.

### Cross-wave carry-forward

- **1. The two invariant-coverage gaps above are the wave's highest-value follow-up, and they are cheap.** See the bullet immediately above for the recommended amendment and for the exactly-two entries that must be re-classified with it. Nothing else in the W3 batch changes as much for as little.
- **2. `RunGitResolver` is STILL OPEN, and W3 did not take it. It should go to W4 or W5, and it needs the fixture W2 specified.** The W2 checkpoint's item 3 recommended "one W3 task builds the git-over-HTTP fixture (dumb protocol: bare repo + `git update-server-info` + static file server) and four scenarios consume it". **That did not happen.** W3's infrastructure task added *object storage* (Garage + an `mc` client + an S3 interposer), which is a different dependency entirely and does nothing for a git-backed job. Nothing in W3 touched `resolveGitPendingRuns`, `ResolveSpec`, or a `git://` `uses:`. **So every W2 finding queued behind that fixture is untouched and carries forward unchanged:** the resolver's per-candidate `ResolveSpec` (a git fetch plus, for private repos, a secret decrypt) running on **every** replica with no lock; the per-replica `failureBackoff` that `docs/operations.md:53` — a *published* paragraph, which is the strongest available limb and better than the unexported comment — says "is held by the current leader only and resets on failover"; the resolve-deadline `AppendLog` that N replicas can each write to one run's log with no lock and no CAS; **W1-7** (AppSource reconciler); **W2-4's** un-run git variant; and **W2-9's** git amplifier and its second, independent hardcoded 50. **Recommendation: W4 takes it if W4 has a task to spare, and W5 otherwise — but it must be the same task that builds the fixture, and it must not be split across waves again.** W3 is the second wave to defer it; a third deferral should be read as a decision to drop it rather than as scheduling. **One thing W3 does contribute:** the object store now exists on `test/ha`, so a git fixture no longer has to bring its own storage, and the `s3proxy` interposer pattern (an nginx sitting between the product and a dependency, stamping `arm=` on every request) is directly reusable as a **git-over-HTTP** interposer — which would give the resolver scenario per-request brackets for free. **CORRECTED AT BRANCH REVIEW — this item forwarded the wrong scope for `docs/operations.md:53` and W4 must read the narrowed version.** That paragraph covers three sweeps, and W3 **did** measure one of them: **W3-2 (`:1736`) falsified both of its clauses live on the log archiver and is filed as a documented-contract violation**, so the archiver's instance is no longer unfiled and must not be re-filed by whoever takes the fixture. What carries forward here is the **git resolver's** instance only — same sentence, different sweep, still unmeasured for want of the fixture — alongside the run-retention sweeper's, which is likewise still code-read. **The measured archiver result strengthens the resolver item rather than replacing it:** the resolver's backoff is per-replica by an *even weaker* mechanism (no lock at all, versus a lock that is merely not sticky), so a scenario that reaches it can now cite a live falsification of the same sentence on a sibling sweep instead of arguing from code alone — see the ledger at `:1758`.
- **3. The `LogPusher` quadratic request amplification remains a W6-S2 input, unchanged by W3, and W3 sharpened the caution attached to it.** W1-6 measured `flushLocked` re-issuing a full `AppendLogBulk` for **every** batch still pending on each 2 s tick, so cumulative volume is triangular — **O(t²) in outage duration, not O(t)**. W3 exercised nothing that bears on the amplification itself, and **W3-5's Part B is not a counter-measurement**: its nine bulk requests over 3 s are a heal-burst, not a sustained outage. It stays owned by **W6-S2**, which must measure request-count *shape* alongside drop-marker frequency and lost-line counts, and the disk-spill decision stays gated on that measurement and must be taken together with the coalesce-the-backlog fix. **W3-5 added the caution that now sits at both ends** (`:474`, `:496`, `:1491`, `:1934`): before any coalescing change is generalised beyond one per-`(run, step)` pusher into an **agent-level** batcher, fix `handleAgentLogBulk`'s scalar `droppedRun` — and note the refinement W3-5 made in its own note, that W1-6's fix **as written** ("coalesce all pending batches", batches not runs) cannot produce a mixed batch and the conflict is with the agent-level generalisation only. **Keep the five `LogPusher`/`AppendLog` shapes distinct when triaging, because W3 added a fourth and a fifth and none overlaps the others:** **F9** is loss at the 1 MiB `p.pending` byte cap (never approached in any W3 arm — `grep -c` over both agents for W3-4's whole session: **0**); the **W1-2 major** is loss at the bounded 5 s step-end `Flush` budget; **W1-6** is pure wasted request volume with no loss; **W3-4** is no loss at all and a permanent *surplus* instead, and is the only one where the **controller**, not the agent, is the party that gets it wrong; and **W3-5** is bytes that arrive, are accepted with a `204`, and are discarded server-side.
- **4. Four escalation calls are left explicitly to the operator, and they are left open on purpose rather than by omission.** Each entry argues its own severity against this file's bands (`:6-8`) and each says where the boundary is. **W3-3** (`:1640`): permanent, silent destruction of secrets written during a mixed window, with no marker on the row, no way to enumerate affected rows and no health surface that goes red — held below critical only because it requires an operator to have already violated a boldfaced, thrice-repeated checklist item. **W3-4** (`:1562`): permanently duplicated *and reordered* stored content served by every read surface, which meets the "silent corruption" disjunct on its text, filed major purely for **calibration** — this file holds zero criticals across 72 entries at the time of filing and the W1-2 major is the mirror-image defect in the same machinery. **W3-1** (`:1950`): the same calibration argument, made explicitly against W1-2, W3-4 and W3-5, with the note that a reader who weighs the docs' own canonical `if [ ! -d vendor ]` cache example may reasonably read it as critical. **W3-6** (`:1866`): `jobs delete` leaks both key spaces for every run of the job on 100 % of invocations, with an automated caller. **The pattern is now stable enough to name: this campaign's `major` band is load-bearing and its `critical` band is empty, and that is a calibration decision, not a measurement.** If the operator escalates any one of these, all four should be re-read together, because each of the four cites the others as its calibration anchor — moving one alone breaks the argument the other three rest on.
- **5. THE AGENT DESTROYS EVERY CONTROLLER ERROR BODY, and this is the single most operationally important fact W3 established for every later wave's *instrumentation*, not just for the product.** `Client.do` replaces the body of every response `>= 400` with the literal `"response omitted"` (`internal/agent/client.go:107-108`), so **no agent-side failure is diagnosable from a run's own logs.** W3-3 measured it at full strength: all 23 secret-fetch failures wrote one identical, content-free line — a `SELECT DISTINCT` over all 23 returns exactly **one** value — while the controller had built a precise message at `api_secrets.go:142` that never crossed the socket. **The consequence for W4-W6 is procedural: capture the controller's `slog` container-side, and do it BEFORE any `force-recreate`, because a recreate erases it** (W3-3 lost controller3's mixed-window WARNs to exactly that and had to note that they were captured beforehand). **Two refinements W3 added and W4 must keep straight, because a fix for one does not fix the other:** `Client.UploadArtifact` does **not** go through `Client.do` — it hand-builds its request and returns a bare `fmt.Errorf("upload artifact http %d", …)` at `client.go:369`, so the body is **never read at all** rather than redacted (W3-2 `:1806`, W3-infra `:1681`); and that is also why the 413 is one of the few agent-side errors that is legible — an operator does get the number. **Two independent mechanisms, same content-free result, two different fixes.**
- **6. W3-6's job-delete entry (`:1864`) needs a measurement before it needs a fix, and it is the only unexecuted item in any wave's violation tally.** The entry specifies it: trigger a job, let a run archive its logs and upload an artifact, `DELETE /api/v1/jobs/{name}`, then `mc ls --recursive` both prefixes. Any rig with an object store does it in minutes, and after W3 every rig has one. **W4 should run it as a warm-up on its first stack rather than filing it forward again** — it costs less than reading this paragraph, and until it is run the wave's second-highest-severity violation rests on a code read.
- **7. W3-6's PRODUCTION window was never attempted — untested, not unreachable — and it is a cheap, high-value W4 arm.** W3-6 produced its orphan with a synthetic agent because that gives a window as wide as the payload and repeatable on demand. The entry then established, as a **code fact** and against its own earlier draft, that an ordinary agent-driven run reaches the same state through documented operator routes with no instrument at all: `handleCancelRun` marks the row terminal **synchronously** (`api_runs.go:374`) while the executing agent learns only by polling at `CancelPollInterval = 5 s`, and the same branch fires for a stuck-run reap and for a failover-driven terminalisation. **So an ordinary run is terminal-while-still-uploading for up to 5 s, and `DELETE /api/v1/runs/{id}` is legal for all of it.** What is genuinely unknown is whether the in-flight `Put` completes before the poller's `cancelRun()` tears down `runCtx` — **nobody tried.** This is the same discriminator W2 carry-forward item 2 asks for on the approval fence (does `cancelRun()` actually kill a long in-flight operation, or does it run to completion?), reached from a second direction, and **the two should be scheduled together on the same arm** because one long-running post-terminal operation answers both.

### Forward questions answered

- **(a) Was I4 actually exercised, and is W2's coverage gap closed? Yes, on both sides, and the per-scenario breakdown is above.** Short form for anyone triaging: **breached** by W3-4 (literal text, three of four clauses) and W3-5 (restated scope); **confirmed to hold, by measurement**, by W3-2 three times over; **not exercised** by W3-3, which says so; **ruled not contradicted, clause by clause**, by W3-1 and W3-6 — and in both of those the reason is that I4's *subject* does not reach the object that was corrupted, which is the coverage gap above and not a pass.
- **(b) Is `RunGitResolver` still open? Yes — see carry-forward item 2.** W3 added object storage, not a git server. Recommend W4, or W5 if W4 has no slot, as one task that builds the fixture and consumes it.
- **(c) What the Garage addition to `test/ha/` means for later waves — three things, and one of them is a constraint rather than a capability.** Task 3 added a Garage service, an idling `mc` client and the `UNIFIED_S3_*` / `UNIFIED_CACHE_*` wiring to `test/ha/docker-compose.ha.yaml` (+108 lines, `0b51fa3`). **(i) Four subsystems that were silently off are now on** — the log archiver, cache cleanup, artifact upload and the agent cache. W2-1's null-result instruction (`:577`) is retired for three of them: a key with zero holders on `test/ha` is no longer "not wired". **The exception is cache cleanup, which is still unobservable** — its ticker's first fire is at t+24 h and no scenario in this campaign lives that long (W3-1 `:2015`); a wave that needs `DeleteExpired` to execute must drive `cache.DeleteExpired` directly from an `edgeprobe` build-tagged test, which is the same recommendation the W2 checkpoint already made for audit retention (`:1503`). **(ii) `docker compose down -v` between scenarios is now MANDATORY, not hygiene** — cache entries, artifacts and log archives live in a dedicated `ha-garagedata` volume and survive a plain `down`, and one leftover `caches/<jobhash>/<keyhash>` object turns a cache-miss expectation into a hit. Every W3 scenario tore down with `-v` and said so in its own entry. **(iii) The constraint: a controller that starts while Garage is unreachable now CRASHLOOPS** (W3-2's own violation, `:1710`), so any scenario that both faults the store and restarts a replica must order the two deliberately. **For W4 specifically (kind):** the compose Garage does **not** transfer — a kind rig needs its own object store or it is back to the pre-W3 state with the same four subsystems off, and W4 should decide that explicitly rather than discover it. **For W6:** the log/NOTIFY hotspot work now has a real archiver and a real seal to race against, which is what made W3-5's finding reachable at all; W6-S2's spill measurement should be taken on a rig with the object store wired, because an unwired archiver means the seal never fires and the drop path W3-5 measured does not exist.
- **(d) The methodological record — this plan's facts block was corrected before the wave began, and then by seven of eight tasks during it.** See the Methodological findings below; the answer to "is the claims-to-check discipline still earning its keep in a third consecutive wave" is **yes, and W3 sharpened it into something more useful than a yes.**

### Methodological findings — four, and all four are wave-level

- **1. The plan's "Verified code facts (do not re-derive)" block was corrected by SEVEN of eight tasks, the third consecutive wave this has happened — but W3-1 offered a refinement that makes the rule actionable rather than merely repeated.** The corrections: **W3-3** overturned "the reason is visible in the run's own logs" (`plan:85`), which was not merely wrong but the opposite of what the path delivers, and overturned "not a contradicted contract" (`plan:88`) by finding `docs/secrets.md:420` at hit #55 of a survey that had been truncated to 40; **W3-5** overturned "structural, every run" for the sidecar seal race on **both** halves (`plan:108`, `:115`) — the ordering is real but the flush wins by microseconds against a 30 s tick, and the "logs have no accommodation" half is wrong because neither log endpoint is an F2 endpoint at all; **W3-6** corrected the artifact-PUT span (`plan:125`, `client.go:349-371` not `:352-360`) and then confirmed the window live; **W3-2** corrected the same span independently and filed it first; **W3-1** overturned `plan:61`'s "step fails → `markFailed`" (the cache path is lenient by explicit written policy and reports `Succeeded`), corrected `plan:59`'s payload-orphan characterisation (it is not dead storage, it is live authoritative content that is **still served**), and corrected `plan:59`'s explanation of the *benign* orphan (the miss came from `:162`, not from the expiry skip at `:244`); **W3-infra** settled `plan:143`'s sidecar question doubly negative and corrected the brief's `test/ha/agent.Dockerfile`, which does not exist; **W3-4** confirmed the `seq`-is-global question the plan flagged (`plan:96`) from the migration, as instructed. **No task left the block untouched, and the plan file now carries `CORRECTED AFTER EXECUTION` blocks in place at `:85`, `:88`, `:108`, `:115`, `:124`, `:125`, `:126` and `:144`** — recorded *and* propagated, which is the distinction W2-3's `:739` drew and which the W2 checkpoint had to admit it got wrong once. **W3-4 is the one task whose corrections were against the brief and the campaign README rather than against the plan**, and both are worth carrying: `sideeffect.payload.json` emits **zero** log lines, not the 120 the brief and `test/edgecase/README.md`'s fixture table both claim (the `echo` is redirected to a file), and the bulk-log URI is `POST /api/v1/agents/{agentId}/runs/{runId}/steps/{stepIndex}/logs/bulk`, not the flat `/agents/*/logs/bulk` the plan sketched — which is why an exact-match nginx `location =` is impossible on that path and the overlay needs a regex `location`.
  - **THE REFINEMENT, AND IT IS WHAT W4 SHOULD ACT ON: it is the block's *mechanism* claims that keep failing, not its `file:line` claims.** Every constant, key, span and line number the block asserted survived checking (three were off by one or two lines and were corrected in place without changing a conclusion). What failed, every time, was an **inference drawn from correct constants** — which is exactly the failure mode the W2 checkpoint named (`:1508`, "the entries most likely to be wrong are the *inferences* drawn from correct constants") and which W3 confirms for the second time. **Two of this wave's planned mechanisms proved not merely wrong but structurally unreachable, and both still produced findings by substitution, which is the other half of the lesson:** W3-5's structural sidecar race cannot exist on this rig at all (sidecars are unavailable by two independent mechanisms) *and* is not structural anywhere, and the scenario substituted **delayed delivery after a partition** — the producer the troubleshooting docs actually name — and hit it on attempt 1; W3-1's chartered delete-mid-restore race is real in the code and **unreachable against Garage v2.3.0**, which delivers an in-flight GET in full after the key is deleted (measured twice, once with no unified-cd and no proxy in the path), and the scenario substituted **severing the connection** and produced the torn extract on the first attempt. **The instruction for W4+: treat the facts block's constants as checkable-in-one-grep and its mechanisms as hypotheses that must be probed before a scenario is designed around them — and when a mechanism turns out unreachable, substitute a fault that reaches the same state, label it a stand-in, and enumerate the ordinary faults that reach it (`:1516`'s discipline, which W3-1 paid in full at `:1984`). A scenario whose planned window does not exist is not a failed scenario.**
- **2. What the process cost, stated plainly, because it recurs and because each failure yields a one-line rule. Four tasks failed something, two of them on spec compliance for evidence traceability.**
  - **Task 3 (W3-infra) quoted four nginx log lines, a `step_reports` table and a duration for the 413 that appeared in NO capture.** The finding was corroborated in the capture, so this was a presentation failure rather than a fabrication — but verbatim-looking log text and a duration must be backed by a capture, so the rig was rebuilt and the whole block re-captured, reproducing the original byte for byte on the message text and differing, as durations do, on the two timings (0.015 s vs an uncaptured 0.012 s; 0.749 s vs an uncaptured 0.863 s). **Rule: if a block looks like pasted output, it must be pasted output — and the capture must exist before the entry quotes it, not after.**
  - **Task 4 (W3-2) stated numbers its own captures contradicted — seven of them, corrected in place at review**: a 59 s archival baseline that was the interval from the *trigger* rather than from terminal (28.4 s); a "~105 s" leader-rotation spacing that appears nowhere in the data (the real spacing is 89-92 s with one 120 s gap); "5" single-process retries where the window boundary gives 4; "≥ 18 s" of lock hold rounded up from a measured 17.46 s; "44-47 s" per failing `Put` against a true 42.8-45.2 s; a `ContentLength` digit quoted from one capture against another capture's citation; and a run census short by two — the two being **exactly the Part B runs later deleted by raw SQL**, i.e. precisely the runs a census exists to catch before deletion removes them. **Rule: re-derive every number from the capture you are about to cite, in the same pass in which you write it — and take the census before anything deletes rows.**
  - **Task 6 (W3-5) filed a re-file of an existing entry.** Its already-ruled check greped `docs/` passages and never greped `FINDINGS.md` for the *finding*, so it re-filed W2-2's cancelled-run-leaves-a-`Running`-step defect as a new I7 entry. It was withdrawn at review and survives as a cross-reference (`:1936`) contributing exactly one thing — a **fourth reproduction, on a controller built with PR #113 present**, confirming `MarkRunStepsInterrupted` still has one caller. **Rule: the already-ruled check has two halves — grep `docs/` for the passage AND grep `FINDINGS.md` for the finding — and the second half is the one that catches a re-file.** W3-1 is the model: its check (`:1973`) greped `FINDINGS.md` too, broke the 21 hits down in full, and corrected its own earlier three-way characterisation of them rather than restating it.
  - **Task 5 (W3-6) claimed a class fully enumerated while a third producer sat unevaluated in its own capture.** Its violation entry said the orphan class's producers "are named here: W3-2's failed compensation and W3-6's artifact-prefix TOCTOU" — and `docs/cli.md:164`, the job-delete leak, was **line 80 of that entry's own docs survey**, printed before the entry existed. It was found at review and spun out as its own entry (`:1864`), which the sibling now names and scopes to explicitly. **Rule: a completeness claim must be checked against your own captures before it is written — and if you find a producer that is worse than the entry you are writing, spin it out rather than folding it in, because folding an unmeasured, windowless, higher-severity producer into a measured race entry makes the entry's severity claim wrong and not just its completeness claim.**
  - **Task 2 (W3-3) piped a docs survey through `head` and did not disclose it** — 40 of 55 hits kept, and hit #55 was the one that turned "the docs are silent" into the wave's second major violation. Caught at review by re-running the identical command and comparing line counts. Both captures are archived (`w3-3/docs-greps-ORIGINAL-TRUNCATED.txt` beside the re-captured `docs-greps.txt`) because how it failed is part of the deliverable. **Rule: never pipe a survey through `head`, and print `| wc -l` beside the output before writing "the docs are silent" into an entry or a plan.** This is now a standing method rule and every later W3 scenario printed its counts (W3-2 231/54/210/27, W3-6 64/169/30/5, W3-5 35/49/84/1, W3-1 97/33/87/11).
  - **Two capped arms never reported their attempt count, against the wave's own explicit rule.** Every W3 runbook says "**report every capped arm's attempt count either way, with the cap stated**", and four scenarios paid it (W3-4 4-of-10, W3-5 1-of-3, W3-6 Part A 1-of-6, W3-2 Part B 2-of-6 and 1-of-6). **W3-3's Part D1 and W3-6's Part C4 each declared a cap and reported no count**, and W3-1's *delete-route* attempts are reported only as "negative" with no number. **Rule: a declared cap creates a reporting obligation that survives a hit on the first attempt — "1 of 6" is the result, not "it worked".** An unreported count is indistinguishable from an unbounded one, which is the whole reason the rule exists.
- **3. The wave's own review found and withdrew two classifications and one contract citation, and all three withdrawals are more instructive than the entries they corrected.** W3-infra's 413 was withdrawn from major violation to minor observation because **this branch had already ruled the other way on the same sentence** in W3-4's Notes and filing the broad reading would have made the branch say two incompatible things about `docs/high-availability.md:253-254`; W3-1's headline was reclassified from violation to observation because it disclaims both violation limbs by its own upheld rulings (see above); and W3-5's I7 re-file was withdrawn outright. **The one-line rule that covers all three, and it extends W2's `:1509`: before filing, grep this branch for every doc line and every finding you are about to cite, and read what it already decided — the campaign is now large enough that its own prior rulings are a source of contradiction, not only the product's docs.**

- **4. A NEW failure mode this checkpoint found, which no previous wave had: the runbooks and `FINDINGS.md` have DRIFTED, always in the same direction — the runbook is the stale side, because review corrections were applied to the entry and not propagated back.** Every W3 runbook carries an execution-notes section with its own results table, and this checkpoint re-read all six against the entries. **Six drifts, none of which changes a measurement, and one of which would have corrupted this tally if it had been trusted.** *(Nothing below is fixed by this checkpoint — the entries are authoritative and the drifts are recorded here so W4 knows which side to read. They are listed in descending order of how much damage trusting the wrong side would do.)* **ALL SIX HAVE SINCE BEEN RESOLVED in a follow-up commit on this branch, which is why the runbooks now carry struck-through superseded text where this list points. This list is kept as the record of what drifted and why, not as a live to-do; no severity, classification or measurement was changed in resolving them.**
