  - **(i) W3-1's runbook states a tally that the file contradicts, and it is the one the checkpoint would have inherited.** `w3-1-cache-delete-vs-restore.md:608-614` says "**2 violations (1 major, 1 minor) and 2 observations (both minor)**". The file holds **1 violation (minor) and 3 observations (one of them major)**, because the headline entry was reclassified at review and the runbook was not updated. **The tally in this checkpoint is taken from the entries, mechanically, and is 8/14/1.**
  - **(ii) W3-4's runbook still instructs a future reader to do work already done.** `w3-4-bulk-append-duplication.md:651-653` and `:855-867` say Part E "**is not measured and must not be written up as if it were**" and "**must be re-run after Task 3**". It was re-run — `FINDINGS.md:1557` carries the AMENDED bullet with the measurement (`line_count=8 max_seq=20`, run `46e21b34`, all 8 records including the 4 duplicates), the labelled caveat that the duplicates were SQL-planted, and the instruction not to cite that sub-bullet as an independent reproduction. **None of that nuance is in the runbook.**
  - **(iii) W3-2's runbook says its Part A outage was "8 min held".** The entry measures the attempt window at `03:03:37Z – 03:18:21Z` (**14.73 minutes**, and the arithmetic at `:1752` depends on that boundary) inside an outage running `03:02:15.838Z → 03:21:54.42Z` (**19 m 38 s**). Eight minutes cannot be reconciled as rounding on either reading; the entry is right and the runbook's step text was not updated when the hold was extended.
  - **(iv) W3-2's runbook says "both Part B arms hit on the first attempt", dropping the qualifier the entry keeps** — "*with that lever*". Arm 1 took **2** attempts against a cap of 6, the first with the `s3-latency` lever that did not work. The entry is right and the distinction is the whole reason `s3-latency`'s large-value failure is on record.
  - **(v) W3-6 states two different end instants for one session, and the file does it to itself.** The Repro line (`:1816`) says "Executed 2026-07-31 `04:36:29Z – 04:49:24Z`" while `:1835` scopes a negative claim against "the whole `04:36:29Z – 04:57:27Z` session". **The capture settles it and neither number is wrong**: `w3-6/teardown.txt` is stamped `04:57:16.881Z` and runs to `04:57:27.724Z`, so `04:57:27Z` is the session and `04:49:24Z` is the *measurement* window, ending with Part C's DELETE return. ~~**Deliberately not edited**~~ — both readings are defensible, `:1835`'s is the load-bearing one and is correct as written, and picking one silently is exactly what a checkpoint should not do, **so the resolution was recorded here first and applied afterwards rather than silently: both lines have SINCE been edited to say which span they name — the Repro line is now labelled the *measurement window* and `:1835` the *session* — and neither figure changed.** **W4: read `:1835`'s figure as the session.**
  - **(vi) W3-6's runbook fuses a client-side duration with a server-side timestamp** — "DELETE 204 in 13 ms at `04:45:28.891Z`" — where 13 ms is curl's and `04:45:28.891Z` is the access log's completion stamp for a **10 ms** handler. The entry keeps them apart. Trivial in size and exactly the class the wave otherwise polices.
  - **The rule for W4+, and it is a merge gate rather than advice: when a review changes an entry, propagate the change to the runbook in the same commit — and at the wave checkpoint, re-read every runbook's results table against the entries before tallying anything.** A campaign whose scenarios and findings are separate documents will drift, and the drift is invisible from either side alone. **`FINDINGS.md` is authoritative; a runbook's execution notes are a record of what was done, not of what was concluded.** (W3-3 is the model for how to handle it when the conclusion moves: its correction banner at `:3-13` and `:65-94` explicitly withdraws the pre-execution framing — though even there the un-struck body text at `:104-121` still reads "a conformance measurement, not a hunt for a contradicted contract", which the scenario's own major violation refutes.)

### Tooling and asset notes carried forward

- **THE ARM RULE, WHICH IS THIS WAVE'S MOST TRANSFERABLE OUTPUT AND WHICH W4, W5 AND W6 ALL INHERIT: a verb is verified when SOME CAPTURE MEASURES ITS EFFECT — not when its comment carries a measurement, and never when "the file is present, `s3-show` lists it, `nginx -t` passed and the `arm=` stamp appears".** `inject.sh s3-slow` was verifiably **loaded** — three independent checks passed — and did nothing at all. The verb table stands verified today, arm by arm: **`s3-block` is the best-verified arm in the table**, because it runs a per-arm effect probe *at arm time* parameterised on the actual method and prefix plus a control that must not be blocked, and W3-2 confirmed it live and prefix-scoped; **`steplock` shares `s3-slow`'s failure class and its own comment admits it** (a silent no-op against the wrong nginx conf) and is effect-confirmed by **execution** rather than by its comment, via W2-5's probe-confirmed 403 and the agent-originated 403s in the nginx log; **`s3-latency`** is the one verb whose comment carries its own measurement. **The standing requirement is forward-looking and is a merge gate, not advice: every NEW or CHANGED arm must ship with an effect measurement — a capture, a probe the verb itself prints, or the comment, in that order of preference.**
- **Rig facts W4-W6 need, each paid for once this wave.** **(i) `docker compose down -v` is mandatory** — the `ha-garagedata` volume is persistent and a leftover cache object turns a miss into a hit. **(ii) The nginx overlays do NOT stack, and there are FOUR of them, not three** — ~~"the three nginx overlays"~~ **corrected at branch review, because this fact is presented as complete and an incomplete "here is the whole set" is worse than no list at all.** Every file mounting `/etc/nginx/nginx.conf` **on the `nginx` service**: `bigbody.override.yaml:31`, `logfault.override.yaml:18`, `steplink.override.yaml:18` and **`oneway.override.yaml:7`** (`nginx-edge.conf` — the W1-5 one-way-partition overlay, still in the tree and still the base every `nginx-block` scenario needs). Compose merges volumes by target path, so **the last file listed silently wins** — silently, which is the whole hazard. **One of the four collisions is deliberate and must not be "fixed":** `steplink` is *designed* to be stacked on top of `oneway` and to override its `nginx.conf` while inheriting its `blocklist` volume (`steplink.override.yaml:1-14`), so `-f oneway -f steplink` in that order is correct. Every other pairing is an accident. **`s3proxy.override.yaml:28` also mounts `/etc/nginx/nginx.conf` but is NOT in this set** — it mounts it on the separate `s3proxy` container, which is why `bigbody` *does* stack with it. **(iii) `s3-latency` breaks a `Put` rather than widening it at large values.** The arm depends on a connect to `192.0.2.1` **hanging**; if the host answers with an ICMP unreachable nginx sees `connect() failed (111: Connection refused)`, which is `error` and not `timeout`, `proxy_next_upstream` never fires, and the request **502s**. Measured at `s3-latency 30`: a log-archive `Put` got `502 … rt=21.037`. The 3-second measurements are not withdrawn — at 3 s the connect deadline expires first — but **a scenario needing a wide window must use `inject.sh pause garage` behind the interposer**, which is a genuine hang bounded only by minio-go's 60 s `ResponseHeaderTimeout`. W3-2 hit both Part B arms on the first attempt with that lever after `s3-latency` failed. **(iv) minio-go retries a 503 ten times internally**, with backoff, so **choose the block status deliberately**: a `503` arm produces a slow retried failure that moves the timing, while `403` fails immediately. W3-2's Part C chose 403 on exactly this ground, because it was measuring a one-shot policy and not a retry policy. **(v) The Windows/MSYS `curl` trap, in both its forms.** W3-6 note 1: an MSYS-form `@`/upload path can upload an **empty body and still return 204** — the gate's first artifact was 0 B and the listing it backed was taken against it, and only the re-upload is the 4.0 KiB object. W3-5's mirror image is **loud**: `curl -D`/`-o` with MSYS-form output paths failed to open them, exit 23, `http_code=000`, and the request never left the client. **Both are avoided the same way: Windows-form paths for every `curl` file argument, and check `%{size_upload}` — never the status alone.** **(vi) Related, and it caught this checkpoint out too:** `%{size_upload}` on a chunked `PUT` counts the on-the-wire bytes **including chunk framing** — W3-6's orphan reads `upload_size=33558547` against an object of exactly 33554432 bytes, and the 4,115-byte gap is framing, not a short write.
- **Smaller rig facts, each of which cost time once this wave.** `MSYS_NO_PATHCONV=1` must be exported before any compose command on Windows (all six runbooks do it, on the W2-5 path-rewrite lesson) — **and it is the direct cause of the `curl` trap above**, so the two are one fact, not two. `docker compose up -d --force-recreate` **erases that container's log history**: capture before you repair, which W3-3 did and says so, and which W3-2, W3-5 and W3-6 all record independently. `docker compose exec` costs ~200-400 ms, so 1 Hz is the practical sampling floor — do not design an arm that needs faster. **The `mc` image has no `sed`, no `ps` and no `grep`**, which cost W3-1 one VOID attempt (a `.meta` rewrite that silently produced an empty body). `inject.sh partition` **silently no-ops** unless the container is on `unified-cd-ha_default`. `DELETE /api/v1/agents/{id}` is an **agent-auth** route and the admin token gets 401 on it. `docker compose logs --since` still lags several seconds (wait ≥6 s, the W2-8 lesson, re-confirmed). **And a `curl -N` sampler launched without `-m` outlives the shell that started it and appears in neither the host `ps` nor `jobs`** — W3-4's teardown caught a live one (pid 169 in the nginx container, killed only by `down -v`), which is why the wave's sampler-hygiene check is now three-part: `jobs`, a host `ps` grep, **and an in-container `ps`**.
- **One standing README recommendation is now stale relative to the scenario it was written for, and W4-W6 will read the README before they read this file.** `test/edgecase/README.md:198` and the `artifact-large.payload.json` row at `:329` still present `s3-latency 3` as "the knob W3-6 should reach for first" for widening a large artifact PUT. **W3-6 as executed used neither it nor the interposer at all**, and for a reason that generalises: the DELETE it was timing itself calls `obj.Delete` and `obj.List`, so pausing the store behind the interposer would hang the instrument alongside the subject. It used `curl --limit-rate` against `proxy_request_buffering off` instead, which its own notes call "a better width knob than anything in `inject.sh` for this race". **The 9.702 s measurement behind the README row is real and is not withdrawn** — it just answers a different question (does `s3-latency` widen a large PUT rather than break it: yes, at small values). ~~**Recorded here rather than edited, because changing a verb-table recommendation is a campaign-asset decision this checkpoint was not asked to make.**~~ **TAKEN — the decision was made in a follow-up commit on this branch.** Both README passages are corrected in place: `:198`'s sentence is struck and rescoped to what the 9.702 s actually measures (`s3-latency 3` *delays* a large `PUT` rather than breaking it), the `artifact-large` row no longer calls it "the knob W3-6 should reach for first", and a **§"width-knob rule for W4-W6"** table was added immediately after the measurement — the place an author reaching for a width knob reads first — routing each side of a race to the knob that was actually measured to widen it: `pause` behind the interposer for a controller-side S3 call, `curl --limit-rate` + `proxy_request_buffering off` for a client→controller upload leg, `s3-slow` for a response-body stream, and prefix padding for the delete loop. **No measurement was withdrawn and no verb was removed from the table.**
- **Instruments this wave added and W4+ should reuse.** The **S3 interposer** (`compose/s3proxy.override.yaml` + `nginx-s3.conf`) is the wave's best instrument: an nginx between the product and Garage, side-selected by bucket (`unified-cd-logs/` is the controller's, `unified-cd-cache/` is the agent's), stamping `arm=` and a status onto **every** request, which is what let five of six scenarios bracket their claims per request instead of per wall clock. It resolves the W2-5 reload trap differently from `nginx-logfault.conf` and better for this path — `keepalive_timeout 0` rather than `worker_shutdown_timeout 1s`, so a reload is deterministic without **killing** in-flight multi-megabyte PUTs and cache-restore streams. The **`mc` service idles with its alias preconfigured** because `exec` costs milliseconds where `compose run` costs a container start, and injection timing matters; note it points at `garage:3900` directly and **bypasses the interposer**, so an arm must be measured through a job, never through `mc`. `tools/w3/fixcheck` parses fixtures through the real `dsl.Parse` offline and every W3 fixture that used it worked on the first API call. **The positive in-flight signal is the pattern to copy** — W3-6 fired its DELETE on `mc ls --incomplete` returning a multipart upload and W3-2 fired its on three consecutive granted `pg_locks` samples; both hit on the first attempt, where W2's unaided repetition scored 0/20 and 0/10.
- **Execution deviations, all six declared by their own runbooks, recorded so a re-runner is not surprised.** W3-2 ran **Part C before Part B**, because Part B deletes `runs` rows with raw SQL that would have polluted Part C's census — the ordering is a rule, not a preference. W3-5 partitioned **agent2**, not the `agent1` its runbook names, because agent2 claimed the run; the arm follows the claim. W3-4 **superseded its Part D1 in place** (SSE through the LB dies at the first arm, because `worker_shutdown_timeout 1s` severs in-flight streams) and re-took the capture directly against `controller1` from inside the nginx container, keeping the dead capture as the counter-example. W3-4 also **deliberately rejected** the brief's `inject.sh pause postgres` lever with three reasons on record — a re-runner should not reach for it. W3-1 **added a Part A'** after Part A's chartered delete route produced a negative, and **added a fixture** (`workloads/cache-torn.{yaml,payload.json}`) the task's file list did not name. W3-6 **skipped Part D** and argued the skip. **The pattern to keep: a deviation is fine and a silent deviation is not.**
- **The rig's intermittent-500 allowance was never spent in ANY of the six scenarios — and every runbook now labels that correctly.** Not one W3 scenario had to retry a step for a spurious 500. **The wording every entry uses is the point: that is an absence of trouble, not a measurement**, because no capture counts 500s across a session. W3-3 sharpens it into a rule the later waves need, because a 500 is its expected *result*: a 500 from `POST /api/v1/runs` is rig flakiness, a 500 from `POST /api/v1/agents/*/secrets/fetch` is the measurement, **and they are told apart by the path in the controller access log, never by the status alone.**
- **Statement logging was armed exactly once this wave, and that is a result.** W3-4 used `log_statement='all'` for one window and it answered the question nothing else could (2558 `INSERT`s, 0 `rollback`). Everything else came from per-request access-log brackets. **The cost comparison is the argument: W2 needed ~96 MB of evidence to answer its questions; W3 needed ~4.9 MB** (the block-allocated `du -sh` figure; the byte sum is **4,582,411 B** — see `:2124`). W2's two statement-log traps still apply when it is needed (pgx logs parameterised queries as `execute stmtcache_<hash>:` and arg-less ones as `statement:`; only the first physical line carries the `%m [%p] h=%h` prefix), as does the one-`ALTER SYSTEM`-per-`psql -c` rule.
- **Evidence archive.** `edgecase-evidence/w3/` now holds all six scenarios plus `w3-infra/` (**~4.9 MB is the block-allocated measure, `du -sh`; the byte sum is 4,582,411 B, `du -sb` — both are stated because the two differ by ~7 % on a tree of many small files and a re-measurer should know which one this figure is**), **all seven directories verified byte-identical against the session scratchpad with `diff -r`**. The wave's one Postgres statement log is stored gzipped (`w3/w3-4/partB-pglog-raw.txt.gz`); the W3-4 entry cites the uncompressed name and its **71,852 lines / 4,964,400 bytes**, which `gunzip -c` reproduces with a matching sha256. **Credentials were scrubbed, and the way this was found is the rule.** W3-1 found a full `uca_` agent credential that W3-5 had left in a dotfile and scrubbed it mid-wave; **W3-6 then made the identical mistake in three files and nobody found it until this checkpoint swept the whole wave** — `.atok`, `.tokline` and `gate-g6-synthagent.txt` each carried the `w36-probe` identity's full access credential, and the last also carried its full `uce_` enrollment token. All are replaced in place by the prefix the entries cite plus a dated `<SCRUBBED …>` banner, both identities were destroyed with their stacks, and a full-token regex sweep over `w3/` now returns zero. **Rule: every wave checkpoint regexes the ENTIRE archive for credentials before copying, not only the scenario that happened to be reviewed.** Four capture-hygiene notes are recorded in the evidence root's own README rather than here, because they are about the captures and not about the findings: two `w3-2` samplers label a cadence they did not achieve (nominal 1 s and 2 s, actual ~1.59 s and ~2.59 s — every duration the entry states is timestamp-derived and unaffected, but "55 of 70 samples" spans ~111 s and not 70 s); **`w3-1`'s workspace samplers are the same class and were missed by the first pass of this list — `partA-torn.txt:15-17` and `partA-attempt1.txt:4-39` were described in the entry as "once per second" and are actually ~2.11 s apart** (`06:59:04.129 → :06.243 → :08.365`), which is why the descriptor is corrected at `:1952` and `:1984`; `w3-6`'s `upload_size` framing gap above, now reconciled inline at `:1820`; and `w3-1`'s two readings of one cache key at 16909275 B and 16909279 B, which is a real re-save between the two reads and not a discrepancy. **All four were checked at this checkpoint and none is an inconsistency between entries** — in every case the load-bearing claim is timestamp-derived or otherwise independent of the mislabelled cadence. *(The original checkpoint added "no W3 entry was modified by this checkpoint"; that remains true of the checkpoint itself, but entries have since been corrected in follow-up commits on this branch — see the drift list at `:2105` and the traceability corrections carried inline above.)*
